"It's time Regina had one. I don't snow what you girls are about. I .vas married a- eighteen, and you're two-and-twenty !-7-but there, I always said you 'were cut out for an old maid."

"Regina .only just knows this young man; he helped,her in an accident---,-" "Oh,. I know," laughed Mrs. Treherne ; "it's so convenient to have an accident when there's a young man handy. I sprained my ankle too, when I was walking out with your father."

"But, mother, I assure "There, _there; what does it matter? Regina is pretty enough, though a sailor ain't the sort for a husband as I know ; but it's no affair of ours." Therewith Mrs.Treherne clattered into the scullery, and began washing the teacups. After that Mary saw Mark and Regina walking about together frequently ; sometimes they would lOok in at her Is. she sewed, with a nod and a smile and a few cheery words of greeting; sometimes they would pass by without glancing up at her window, appar- ently in eager conversation. On those days Mary felt a tightening round her heart and an inexplicable sadness. She was glad, of course,- very glad of her friend's -happiness, for it was plain she liked Mark, and that he liked her; and yet Mary.would have preferred a share in it • too, jtist the smile, the look, the few light words that contented her for the day. '

On one occasion the sailor's bright face appeared

alone at the window.

nlffy I come in?" he said. "I shall be going back to sea soon, and I want a chat with you." "Certqinly ; come in ; but where is Regina ? " "Oh, I don't,know; we are not inseparable, you know."

"I thought--" began Mary, and she stopped.. "You thought—yes, I can see it in your eyes-- you thought we were sweethearts." "Perhaps I aid," said Mary lightly, her heart springing up with a new elation. ".No, no, I assure you,.". and the young man, seating himself, looked firmly into_ Mary's face; "we are only friends. We heard there had been some talk in the village, so we've sworn ,off for a bit."

Mary curled her lip. "Wasn't that rather cowardly—if you both love one another ? "

"Bless you, but we don't ; we're just friends!" "You Feem to have a great deal to say to one

another."

"So we had; a sailor on shore is always a bit lively; but I'd like to say something to you if I dared."

"You can say it," answered Mary, in a very owl voice.

'—the sailor's bright ." and it makes a man

"You're such a quiet girl " face suddenly grew serious— shy like, when he wants--"

"Wants what ?". said Mary, carefully turning

Ler eyes away. "When he loves her—and wants to tell her Sc." "Loves her ? " Mary glanced at him helplessly.

"You'll think it a bit sudden, but ever since the first day I knew you were the girl for me. I langl.ied and jokes with Regina, and talked a lot of nonsense, but all the time I thought of you so good, so pretty, sewing quietly at home. Could you care for me, Mary? "

"Could I? " Mary's eyes laughed out loud. "I'm a bit rough, just only a. common sailor, though I shall be mate soon; and you're so dainty, like a bit of china; but there, I'll snake you a good husband, I will." He put his arm round her as he spoke, and Mary's head rested lightly on his shoulder. Suddenly she startecl up. "What will Regina say? I couldn't behave false like to her." "She will say nothing ; she knows," and then Mary's head settled down contentedly again on the big bread shoulder. The news of Mary's engagement startled the village considerably. Only a common sailor, and they had thought Mary aspired so high! Gossip ran riot; there was food and enough for chatter. The wedding was to be at once—indecent haste, people called it—for the young man was due back at his ship in a fortnight. One bright May morn- ing, when the trees were clad in a tender livery of green, and the thrushes and larks clamoured harmoniously overhead, with the scent of the lilacs and wallflowers in the gardens rising to heaven like incense, Mary was married in the village church. She word a light Llue and white dress, and a white chip hat with forget-me-nots, and when she walked dowri the aisle on her handsome husband's arm _a quiet, ecstatic happiness beamed in her sweet face. After the ceremony they went back to tea at Mary's cottage, where Mrs. Trelserne presided stolidly over a feast of Cornish good things. Pies and cakes and clotted cream, tea for the women and beer for the men; sent the guests away merrily enough. Then, in the warm evening air, Mark and ,his bride stood in the narrow garden among the lilacs and the may blossom, and, hand-in-hand looked out over, the sea.

"'Tis there I shall be soon," said Mark; "see, you can just note a ship in full sail passing along. There's some dangerous rocks on that coast, too."

• 'Mary trembled slightly, and drew her husband down to her. "If only you had not to go away! " she said softly. - " 'Tis hard," he said regretfully; "but. - there, the times Will be all the happier when we are together. You'll think of me sometimes? " "Every hour of the day, Mark; and at eventide I'll come out here and look at the sea and wonder where you are."

"That's right, lass—every evening, and I shall know you're looking for me, Mary, my Sweet little quiet woman." They could hear the ripple of the waves falling gently against the beach, but both knew, though neither mentioned it, how cruelly those innocent little waves flung and battered themselves. and foamed agaiiist the granite rocks in times of storm and tempest. It was quiet enough now, the sky overhead was of one untroubled blue, stars gleamed and twinkled, and the distant sounds of a fiddle at the public-house fell mellowed on the air. "They're dancing," said Mark thoughtfully. "I'm fond of dancing, and can foot h hornpipe with anybody, but I don't feel like that to-night; it all seems so hushed and holy like out here, Mary, my wife."' Then, after a pause. he added: "Let us go in." A few days later Mark left his newly-made bride in the cottage with her mcther, where she con- tinued to sew and to sit in her window just as before.

Only the bright gold wedding-ring on her finger and various small, vaguely spelt, and much thumbed letters with foreign post-marks, which reposed next to her bosom, told of the quickly-won and quickly-lost husband. Mary's heart ached a little sometimes, but she remembered Mark's last words: "Be brave, dear, as a sailor's wife sl;ould Le," and she forced the rare mile to her eyes and the lagging words of welcome to her lips when Regina called or a neighbour came in for a talk. The village mind was scarcely certain whether to pity or congratulate the young wife. It had all been so hurried, so sudden, so strange, and village people are stolid folk, and require plenty of time in which to grow accustomed to novelty. . Mary herself never allowed that there was any cause for pity. She prefacel all remarks INjth "When Mark comes back," "The first thing Mark will do," or "In Mark's last letter he said," an, ...vary evening regularly she went out into the little garden, and shading her eyes, looked wistfully over the.sea, that sea she was growing to love with kind of mysterious awe and wonder. Sometimes it glistened like a looking-glass in the sunshine, sometimes it shone in wondrous stillness under the rays of the moonbeams, sometimes it swayed and heaved like a soul in trouble, and in the winter evenings it moaned and foamed and beat against the rocks like some wild guilty spirit, Those were

A SAILOR'S BRIDE.

BY LADY VIOLET GREYILLE,

Author of "Mrs. Weston's Grandchild," "A Mere Acquaintance," "Nurse Ciceley's Renuncia-

tion," &c.

Mary Treherne was the prettiest girl in the village, a small, straegling, picturesque fishing village on the coast of Cornwall.., Mary was a dressmaker, and had received a good education. As the expounder of fashion and the pioneer of new and modern graces, ie flavour or hung about her, which resolved itself into silent admira- tion when she appeared in church. Not that Mary was ever over-dressed, or whet is vulgarly called smart. .Mary was too wise for that. She wore a pretty straw hat trimmed with forget-me-note and a plainly-made blue-and-white Fria dress in the summer, and a dark blue serge or cashmere dress in the winteee Blue was her favourite colour. It was also the cl'our of her eyes, modestly veiled..by long dark lashes. Mary's cottage 'stood on the bill at the top of the winding street, and a lovely view could be gained therefrom. The little garden Was a blaze of sunflewers, roses and hollyhocks ; roses clambered lovingly over the walls, and a bed of mignonette close to the gate shed sweet odours all around. Mary was a very happy girl. Her tastes were simple; she /cited her work, and sang over it all day;. she took her part in the choir in. church, and walked out with her friend Regina' Pendarves on Sunday. Neither of the two had a lover yet, though both in their way were attractive. Mary was quiet tall and slight, with the-pallor of complexion due to leong hours in a workroom, and had masses of rich i nut-brown hair to match her lashes. Regina was all roses and sunshine, her hair like spun gold, her cheeks rosy, her clear grey eyes as limpid as the waters of a brook. A few people thought Regina the prettiest girl in the village, but the general verdict was in favour of Mary. Regina's father 'was a rich man; he owned a large farm, and drove a gig'; while Mary 'kept herself and her mother, and worked herd for a living. 'Though they had not yet chosen a sweet- heart, both girls found plenty of admirers, anxious , to wile away a spare half-hour .in talking .love nonsense, or looking into elicit., lerghe eyes. 'But Mary ,fancied none or! them; neither the grocer's sone—a promising young man with a keen eye to business—nor the schoolmaster, who wanted, an educated soul in harmony with his own; nor even the banker's clerk, who wore doeskin gloves and looked quite the gentleman:- Mary aspired too high, the village gossips said. "Was it likely that she would make a better match? Mrs. Earnshamerlhe portly matron at the public- house, shook her head reprovingly, while Mrs. Jones at the tallow-chandler's shop said she didn't know whet girls were coining to. It was all that Board school education, she supposed, and a habit of playing the piano—for Mary played quite nicely, and in the summer evenings the sound of her music strayed fitfully across the street on the warm breeze. Meanwhile Mary, unmoved, sewed away steadily, and indulged in no dreams of the future. She put all her energies in the direction of gored skirts and puffed sleeves, and felt a proud woman indeed the day that Miss Grace, the young lady from the Mancr, brought her a new pattern of odice and asked her to copy it. Miss,Grace took a fancy to the quiet young seamstress ; she asked her-up to the Manor House Once cr twice after that, on the plea of consulting her about the set of a bow or the style of a trimming. She gave her fee in the drawing-room, am* the old china and the valuable prints and the softly-shaded green and-white chintzes. Mary looked eagerly at all the pretty things; dressmaking had developed in her a dis- tinct artistic taste ; and rumour assert esi that Miss Grace's brother, a young mart fresh home from Oxford, On his-rart, looked a little too curiously at Mary during these interviews. However, nothing can of them, except Mary's renewed interest in her work, and time passed uneventfully. One day it was reported that Rr gine had been seen walking with a young sailor, who was bronzed and handsome; next day they were seen bending their steps towards Mary's cottage. Mary was at work, but she looked up pleasantly at, her friend's entrance. She practised a habit of never giving up her occupation, ;:.even though Regina often lounged in, being a young lady with- out especial employment, who found time hang heavy on her .hands. So to-day Mary continued sewing, her slim fingers moving to and fro quickly. Regina ietrOduced the young sailor. "He picked me up the other day when I fell off my bicycle," she said gratefully, "aria his name is Mark Munson, just home from China." . Mary rooked at him with interest. "How many things you must have seen! I have often wished to visit foreign lands." The sailor bent forward eagerly. "We do see many things, _pleasant and curious, terrible and sad, but the best of all are at home," and the young man glanced with evident admira- tion at the two sweet girl faces. Regina .blushed a little. Her skin was very trans- parent, and the blood rose, easily to her cheeks. Mary remarked it, and wondered. "Do you stay here long? " she began. "No; only while our ship is paid off. We shall go to sea again soon. I'm down here on a visit .tolney father and mother at Boscastle—you know the town—and they sent me with a message to Miss Regina's father about business." "And we met, and you helped me with my bicycle," put in Regina. "We11,.1 don't know much about bicycles; the Seagull—that's the name of our ship, miss—is more id my line; I can handle a rope better than

a machine." "And yet you mended it quite beautifully 1" averred Regina. • "Sailors learn to be handy with their fingers," he responded mcodestly. Mary- watched the brilliant flush that again passed over the mobile countenance of her friend. Pestches of sunshine came through the open door, and danced amongst the leaves of the geraniums in the window; the air was warm and soft, and a thrush sang loudly in the lilac-bush. A strange oppression seemed to strangle Mary. She rose uneasily, and touched some shells on the mantelpiece. ,"Father brought these home once," she said; "be was a sailor too." "I have seen shells like those," remarked Mark; "they came from the Pacific, ,where the coral islands are. I have sonic bits; if you'll allomme I will bring them over some day for you to see." Mary thanked him, and Regina, declaring she had not time to stay for tea, hurriedly shook Lands. Mary stood at the door as they descended the hill, Regina turning back to kiss her hand, and Mark waving his straw hat. Mary stood till they were out of sight, then she ttirnedj and with

a sigh resumed her work. Never till to-day had

she thought of her friend except as a riotous, mrry girl; but to-day there was a softness in her wanner, a gentleness in her look, that revealed her in the new light of womanhood. The sailor was good-looking too; they would make a handsome couple. Mary meditated, and the neglected work dropped into her lap. She was aroused by her mother's voice in UM back kitchen. "Mary I Mary! what's the matter? Aren't you coming to tea? Why, the water's been on the boil this half-hour!" Mary rose, folded her work (it was a dress for Miss Grace), and went slowly into the back kitchen. "So you've had visitors—Regina and a sailor • •her young man, I s'pote " said Mrs. Treherne, a stout woman with a heavy jaw, who lapped her .±43, from the saucer with the gusto of an epiouro-

The Examiner, Saturday, July 24, 1909,

the worst times for Mary. Often and often she would rise in the night, shuddering at the sound of the sea, and throwing open her casement, catch the salt spray on her cold fa6e, and feel the icy wind .chilling her heart. The dawn found her some- times on her knees, praying, feverishly, fervently, for the safe home-coming of her husband. December he wrote that the ship was returning, and she ,might look for his arrival any day now. The month broke stormier and wilder even than uswAl. Rumours of wrecks and disasters on the rocky coast blanched Mary's cheeks and made her fingers tremble over her work. The lifeboat ha&. been out many times already, and the well-known,' shout when it was being manned sent Mary on. many a futile run down to the beach. Distance, anxiety, and hope deferred had deepened and strengthened her love, until it seemed as though all life were summed up in the one ward—waiting.l Among the fishing-boats misfortunes were frequent, • and a widow and three orphaned children mourned the father and bread-winner. Regina, with herN bright, pretty ways, went about among the fisher population, cheering, helping, with a kind word.. for all.

Mary no longer courted her friend's company; Regina reminded her too poignantly of her lost, happiness—that happiness she was engaged in clutching feverishly and hugging to her lonely bosom. -Reia, however, would not be ignored; she caine and sat with Mary very frequently, and talked to her gaily of her swains and her schemes, her troubles and her joys. One wild December night the two girls sat over the fire talking. Suddenly the loud boom of a gun startled, them. Mary flew to the window. "Rockets !" she said, as a bright stream of light illuminated the --pitchy sky. "A ship in distress l—a big one, too! Regina, let us .go and see what can be done for. the poor people." "This awful night !—why, not a ship could live near those rocks! " Then, seeing Mary pale to the lips and steadying herself feebly against a chair, she added: "But I dare say it's nothing serious this time; perhaps only a fishing-boat." "It was a ship, -I toll you," said Mary hoarsely, as she snatched up a shawl and ran out of the cottage. Regina followed, and both girls quickly bounded down the hill, joining the groups of people that poured out of the houses. The life- Lcat was hurriedly being manneu when they reached the shore, but the wisest and oldest of the crew shook their heads.

"We'll never reach them with such -a sea as this! Poor fellows, I warrant they're doomed.," Three times the gallant crew essayed to reach the ship, and three times they were beaten back. The rockets and signals continued, and in the lurid glare of the flashes hopeless men might be seen clinging to the rigging. "'Tis a big ship—a merchantman," said.: the coastguardsman.

"Does anybody know her name ? " asked Mary, with parched lips. She was trembling with cold, for the two lightly-clad girls stood side by side, motionless with horror, as each futile attempt at saving the crew was renewed. Mary's question met with no answer. Everyone was intent on the ropes which were flung into the seething eddy as a ast resource. Two' of the crew were bold and tortunate enough to reach the shore; then came silence, and in the cold breaking morning light the bosom of the sea was seen to be empty of life. The ship had disappeared. The two survivors told their story. The Seagull —that was her name—bad stranded on the rocks during the violent gale, and a very short time sufficed for her to founder with all hands on board. The Seagull—that was Mark's ship. They. lroke the news to Mary as gently as they could, Regina holding her bands the while. At the doleful news she gave one scream, piercing and terrible, pregnant with. the agony of a despairing soul; then she, fell into a prolonged and deadly faint.

S • •

Weeks passed by; .spring was at hand; the little buds unfolded again, the larks began to sing, and Mary once 'more went about her daily work. She had drawn a curtain over the window whence could be obtained a view of the sea, and she wore black —the rigid, hard, uncompromising black of the widow. She rarely spoke now, rareky left the cottage, and ,listened listlessly to her mother's endless grumbles. Regina herself ~was engaged to be married to a young farmer, and could spare less time to comfort her friend. Mary withdrew more and more within herself, and passed almost whore days without uttering a syllable. Her pretty bright eyes grew dim from crying, her brown hair WA3 streaked with grey. "Poor bride !" the village people said, as. they noted her stony face; "poor bride ! already a widow." About six months later Mary sat, as was her wont, close to the window looking on to the road. Her head hung down, a sad droop , bent her shoulders, her eyes wore the hard, compressed expression that characterised them of late. A gentle knock at the outside door caused her to start and look up. A thin, pale spectre of a man resting on a stick stood outside. "A tramp," thought Mary, and her kindly heart impelled her to oharitable action. "He looks hungry," and she glanced again at her visitor. Then she hurried into the kitchen, cut a thick chunk of bread-and-cheese, and returned to the doorway, holding it in her hand. "Poor fellow! " she said. "Are you a sailor?" The man bit his lips under his shavy, unkempt beard, and mumbled something. "Perhaps you've been shipwrecked," she added; "poor soul, you've suffered sadly ! " Her voice bore the kindly, gentle inflection of one who has become indifferent from pain. The sailor looked ur, and his eyes met hers. Suddenly something seemed to melt in her Cold, resigned heart, a wave of new, living love passed over her. She put her hand up to her eyes. "Mark !is it you—you—come back from the grave ? " In another moment her arms were round his neck, and slle was sobbing, crying, talking and laughing all in one breath. -

iln't I tell you, dear, to bear up and be brave? " he said tenderly, pressing her closer to him; "it's all come right now, but you've a poor, useless, battered husband to take care of. I was saved by a-miracle, and I've been ill all three months."

"Never mind "—Mary's eyes glowed with loving rity—"never mind. I can be brave now, and both thank God together." . "And you'll take oft those black clothes, Mary, and put_ on the blue-and-white dress you were married in, and look like my own dear bride

again ? " Then he kissed her and once more she taste4l II a

fulleA happiness of love. [THE Esp.]

Great Northern's New Route to London.

We are informed that the Great Northern Railway Company are about to open up direct communication between Warrington and London. The advantages of the route and the attractions of the journey are so obvious that it seems strange this line of travel has not been used before now. The traveller leaves War- lington from the Central and joins the Great Northern express that leaves Manchester for King's Cross. The ride across Yorkshire and through smoky Sheffield has its interesting features, but it is after the Great Northern line is entered upon at Retford and pro-: ceeds to skirt beautiful, fertile, and. historical East Anglia that the visitor from the North West of England becomes entranced. Such a journey knows no tedious moments, especially for the traveller who is breaking 'new ground: Besides, the train is furnished with first rate dining cars, and by the time the train reaches the Metropolis at 7.46—n journey of less then five hours with only at Manchester, Sheffield and Granthzim— the /traveller has dined and is ready , for either business or pleasure.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

VIOLENT PRISONERS.

At Widness, on Uonday, two men of the loitering class named Fred. Ford and James Fahey were charge:l with being drunk and disorderly and assaulting Police- !constable Leland, and also with assaulting William Harthorne, of Harris-street. Ford, who bad a very had record, was sent to prison for nine months with hard labour, and Fahey was similarly dealt with.

GALLANT RESCUE AT RUNCORN.

While a number of children were playing on the bank ottlie Bridgewater Canal at Runcorn, on Wednesday `eittuaing, a boy named Kirkham, aged six years, whose 116tne is in Norfolk-street, fell into the water. The boy had 'sunk Several times, when Mr. Flank Hayes, of Storestreet, came upon the scene, and plunging into the water, brought the unconscious boy to the side. Artificial respiration was applied, and the by was tikn to Mr. Hayes's house, where he was strippei and lly restoted. This is the fourth life 111 r. Hayes's Hayes has saved, and his conduct on this occasion is he more gallant, as he is a patient of Liverpool Eye 1 Ear Infirmary, and has been from work for eix .eks. •

DEFECTIVE SCHOOLS. Medical Ulcer Reports on Sanitary

Conditions.

In his first report to the Education Committee an the subject of school hygiene, Dr. J. Coote ilibbert,medical officer of health, has• some striking things to say with regard to the sanitary conditions of the schools of Warrington. Dealing with the general structure of school premises,the Doctor says :—Many of the schools are by no means recent structures and certainly do not comply with modern ideas on school construction. In several, instances the buildings•were not, I believe, originally intended as schools ; and even in the schools, built as such, the requirements for parochial purposes were chiefly considered and the sani- tary defects characteristic of the parent build- ing were often copied. Hence the windows so constructed that they do not open; and hence in sone cases in( fficient lighting. In many instances they are surrounded by high buildings, which obstruct both light and ventilation. A very frequent defect in. ventilation is that cub- a small part of each window can be opened. Ventilatie nis thus often deficient, and it is moreover impossible to flush out the vacated rooms with a free current of fresh air during the intervals for play. In many of the scboo`.s additional air inlets such as Tobin tubes as well as additional air outlets such as Boy le.s ventilators or other extracting shafts, art; urgently required ; and in very many schools a far larger proportion the whole, (if possible) of the window area should be made Ito open. Without adequate ventilation of the schoolrooms, the general health of teachers and scholars deteriorates, while infectious disease finds the most favourable conditions for its spread. "Colds" are often attributed to draughts, but—and I would especially submit this point to the teachers thenuelyes—" colds ' are not so much caused by fresh air admitted from, without as due to the polluted atmoiphere of the ill- ventilated interior. Not only from tte point of view of the child's physical welfare is fresh air a constant necessity, hut it is also impossible fur a child to derive full benefit from the proffered teaching yhen the brain is dulled with the poisons of a stuffy atnfosphere. The greatest blot tie sanitation of certain schools in Warringtcn is to my mind the existence of pail-closets as the so-called stnitary convenienCes. In 14 schools pail-closets are ink use. Dr. Ilibbert refers in some detail to the medical inspection of school children. During 1908 a total of 968 children were examined, and in 30l cases the parents were present at the inspections. One hundred and thirty-nine parents were notified of defects in their children by letter. The main points are the large pro- portion of children with the•following conditions :—(1) Verminous condition of head, 22.6 per cent. of children examined ; (2) defective eyesight (errors of refraetion), 14.0; (3) defective teetb, 22.3; (4) enlarged tonsils, 9.0. After dealing with the question of notifiable diseases, the work of the school nurses Scc., the doctor concludes: To may mind, the n_ain necessity at present seems to be the improvement as far as possible of the sanitary con- ditions of the various schools, and the provision of such further school accommodation as will prevent the undue crowding together of the children in the class- rooms. At the end of the present year (1909) figures referring to the facts revealed by medical inspection and of sufficient magnitude to justify the drawing of definite conclusions will be laid before yeti, and these may suggest to you further action as regards the pro- vision of facilities for the treatment of the various defective physical cm nditior s found amongst the school children. Meantime information will be obtained as to the extent of which parents themselves can be expected to take action after the defective conditions in their children have been pointed out to them.

At the Liverpool Assizes on Monday, George Beard (25), labourer, admitted hirg stolen a post office auk. book, and fcrged an order for the payment of £25. The prosecution was conducted by Mr. Butler, who explainEd that the prisoner and a man named Thomas Johnson were members of a travelling circus, and they slept together in a tent. On the 24th May prisoner took from a box hi the tent a post office bank-book, in which wet() entEred Jchnson's kavir gs d•tring the last two years, amounting altogether to about £32. Prisoner went to the post office at Newton-le-Willows, and by producing the bank-book and- by signing a post office order in. the name of Jchnson, Le' obtained £25. Sentence of six months' impritorrnent with hard labcur was passed.

GRATIFYING EXAMINATION RESULTS.

Mr. Upson called attention to the results of the examinations held by the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes which were highly satisfactory. The number of distinctions gained by the students was nearly double what it was in the preceding year, and Progress was being made in other directions as far as the Technical School and Branch Technical Schools were concerned. Mr. Upson also commented upon the result of the preliminary examination for the certifi- cate (1909), so far as it affects the pupil teachers in attendance at the committee's classes. They sent in 20 students and 20 passed. (Hear, hear). Of those eighteen had secured admission into Training Colleges. He saw from the papers that the Chairman of the Blackpool Education Committee had been swaggering very considerably about the results of the examina- tions in Riad/pool. They did not swagger in War- rington. (Laughter). It was altogether contrary to the humility of the Warrington, mind. (Renewed Itanghter). At any rate they felt a little gratifict.tion in being ahle to present such a favourable result of the examination in Warrington. (Hear, hear). There was °ne striking success, as one student bad gained six dis- tinctions, namely, Miss Dorothy Johnson. PROPOSED EXTENSION OF THE SCHOOL OF ART.

The School Management Committee submitted the following minute :—,On the recommendation of the sub-committee appointed to inquire into the accommo- dation and staffing of the School of Art,it was resolved (a),that steps be at once taken for the enlargement of the School of Art; (b), that the advice of the architects of the school be sought as to the best means of carrying out this extension of the building; (c), that an addi- tional assistant master, specially qualified in furniture, crafts and design, wood-carving and metal-work, be aPpointed at a commencing salary of £lOO per annum; and (d), that the terms of the engagement of the Head Master of the school (Mr. Clarence Alawson, A.R.C.A.) be amended so as to provide that his salary shall be a fixed amount—commencing on the Ist August, 1909, at the rate of £250 per annum, and rising thereafter by an n ual increments of £25 to a maximum of £390. Mr. UPSON said the year before Mr. Mawson was aPpointed 67 students sat for examination. During his fllt year 108 sat and last year 180. There had been 'ncreases in the day and night students and in every one (If the classes held there,so that they were penalized by the success of the school. They were bound to enlarge if they wished to make adequate accommodation for the Students who were already at the school. Mr. 1111wson had rapidly acquired a reputation as a teacher of art in the town and district, and they anticipated that the list glories of the school would be renewed and increased In the immediate future. Then the rapidly growing number of students required additional help in the way of teaching, and it was, therefore, Proposed that an additional assistant master ehould be appointed with special qualifications ler the special needs of some of the art students. The Committee had fixed Mr. Mawson's salary at £250 to is by annual increment of £25 to a maximum of £3OO. That, for the present, did not represent an increase, as hitherto Mr. Mawson bad been paid to some extent by the number of papers worked at the Examinations. ho Board of Education very properly said he ought to /lave a fixed salary. With the magnificent work Mr. atawson had done, the enthusiasm he had displayed and t„ he growing reputation of the school the salary was 0Y no means too large. (Hear, hear). The Rev. Father WILSON seconded. Mr. It. T. (Hear, hear.) He was delighted with 'the figures which Mr. Upson had given. He had a great pride in the School of Art, and he was glad it was 'eturning to the palmy days. "EAR-MARKING" LAND.

Mr. FAIRCLOUGTI proposed the confirmation of the minutes of the Sites and Buildings Committee, and said the members present would see with great satis- faction the decision to reserve the land at Grace's Pottery for the purpose of a new school. It would be a good thing for the committee to ear-mark other land in different districts of the town, so that they could build schools at the proper time, because there was no doubt that if they kept. deferring the question, they would have to pay more money for sites at a future time.

Councillor WILKINSON seconded. and said it had been a source of trouble to several members of the Sites and Buildings Committee that they bad not the power to e ir-mark certain pieces of land which they knew, as Warringtonians, would be wanted during the next five or ten years. The site of Grace's Pottery was already owned by the Corporation, and there w ould be no trouble in transferring it to the Education Committee. Mr. PLINSTON 'wished to know if they were ever going to build schools on the laud they had already acquired? Mr. FAIRCLOUGII pointed out that certain notices had to be given before a building could be erected. Notices bad been printed in the papers, and when the notice bad expired they could proceed, but not till then. Councillor DICKENSON wished to know whether the inspector had refused to allow them to build a School on Grace's Pottery. Councillor WILKINSON said seine years ago the com- mittee in their wisdom, or otherwise, thought Grace's Pottery would be a site for the building of a school in the future. The inspector, who came from London, and who therefore linew more about the district than even he (the speaker) did —(laughter)--said they had no right to anticipate. He (Councillor Wilkinson), how- ever, thought they had a perfect right to anticipate. They could tell what would be required ten years hence better than a man from London. The minutes were carried.

MEDICAL INSPECTION.

The report of the Scheel Medical Officer to the Education Authority for the year 1908 having been con- sidered by the Medical Inspection Committee it was resolved that the Director of Education and the School Medical Officer be requested to prepare, for the con- sideration of the committee, draft recommendations to the managers of those schools whose premises are reported to be defective, with a view to ascertaining whether such defects would be remedied by the managers.

Mr. JOLLEY hoped that the members had "read, marked end inwardly digested "the report prepared by Dr. Ilibbert, as it was a document which should be seriously considered. There were things which were not creditable and the sooner the stigma which rested on the town in the ground for Dr. Hibtert's just criticisms was removed, the better it would be for the credit of the committee. A NEW DISQUALIFICATION.

The General Purplses Committee recommended that no teacher or salaried official of any school or educa- tional institution within the county borough be deemed eligible for appointment as a selected member of the Education Committee. This had been passed in com- mittee by 11 votes to five.

The minutes were proposed-by the Chairman and seconded by Councillor Barton. _ _

Alderman BENNETT proposed the recision of the minute. He thought they had adopted a foolish policy in passing a resolution in which they were deliberately cutting themselves off from the opportunity of avail- ing themselves of the expert knowledge and experience which teachers in certain schools possessed. He even thougbt it a very fatuous policy when they realized bow much the work of the committee owed to the power they had to get expert advice and expert assistance from gentlemen who had special knowledge on the subjects they bad to deal with. Let them take the case of Mr. Upson. If they carried out the resoln- tion to its logical conclusion, they would be deprived of the services of Mr. Upson. Mr. ROBERTS: No! No!

The CHAIRMAN : Mr. Upson is not a selected member. He is co-opted. Alderman BENNETT insisted that if the resolution were carried to its logical conclusion, they would not have the services of Mr: Upson. (No ! No !) The principle was just the same. (No ! No!) He was not objecting to the two names now suggested, because he considered.they were eminently suitable, but it was the principle they were adopting. He was called an idealist, but he was infinitely more practical than many of the gentlemen who jeered at his ideals. He proposed that the resolution be not passed. Mr. FAIRCLOUGR seconded. _

_ 7 Councillor ROBERTS said ample provision was made in the first instance for the due representation of educa- tional and other bodies by the constitution of the Committee and he submitted, with all respect to the profession, that they might have too much of the school master on the Education authority. They had to deal with many questions for which they needed the assibt- ance of practical business men. It would be unfair that one body should have double the number of representatives they were entitled to. The amendment was then put and was defeated by a lair majority.

THREE MONTHS IN INDIA, SYRIA, AND PALESTINE.

By Truda H. Crosfield. (ARTICLE III.)

animals with nuts, in the outer court yard of the shrine. I never saw better climbing than was performed by on infant monkey, whose tiny wizened face expressed the live- liest fear at our approach. It swung from corbels and 'up and down perpendicular carvings in a manner fit to make an acrobat die of envy. Near by, a large cage stood in a garden and seemed to be the object of the villagers' evening walk. We approached, to see a magnificent tiger enclosed in a space barely twice his own length. The poor brute had grown fat and lazy, and as we watched, a girl of twelve or fourteen appeared with the inevitable kerosene tin which does duty for innumerable things in India. Tilting it through the wide bars of the tiger's cage, she poured his evening drink into a pan inside. Her hand and arm were within the cage, but the tiger evidently looked on her as a friend. He crouched down delightedly by the pan, and purred like fifty sewing-machines all going at once!

===== 0002601_19090724_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SACRED CITY OF THE HINDUS.

Benares town is so crowded and oriental that no European could live with comfort in it. We stayed at Clarke's Hotel, two miles out, a quiet low range of buildings with pretty creepers along the white pillars of the verandah. It was the festival of Ganesh, a favourite 'god, who is invoked to bring good luck to all undertakings; and we met thousands of natives returning from the temples and wearing marigold wreaths. But it is sad to see how symbolism has sunk with them to ridiculous and degraded idol- worship; their present rites are a far cry indeed from the lofty ethical purity of the Vedas, the ancient Hindu teachings. One is thankful, at least, that their religion strictly enjoins ablutions; a good teaching, which no doubt keeps down much sickness, other- wise inevitable. Thousands flock constantly to Benares to bathe in the Ganges, here especially sacred, and early next morning we drove down to the ghats and sat on two wicker chairs on the roof of a boat, from whence to witness the bathing as we were slowly rowed up the river and back again.

===== 0002601_19090724_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BATHING GHATS.

It is a marvellous spectacle, and speaks volumes for the piety- of the Hindus, for it was a chilly January morning, and India can be very cold. I would not have bathed

===== 0002601_19090724_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GHATS (BATHING STEPS) BENARES.

for much, yet elderly people as well as young were quietly going into the water by the thousand. Their bright coloured robes made vivid splashes of saffron yellow, pink, crimson and white on the sunburnt steps of the ghats. The ablutions include not only themselves but their clothes, for they go in in one robe, and cleverly slip a dry one over it on emerging, whilst the wet one falls to the ground. I must here say what a pleasure it was to see the bright and beooming colours which the Hindus wore; and I could not help regretting that we English folk did not wear brighter clothing, instead of being addicted on the whole to blacks, greys and browns Further up the ghats, two small fires of wood were burning, and as we approached, we clearly saw brown arms and legs projecting from amongst the branches of which the fire was composed. The little morning breeze was blowing the low smoke away,from us, of which I was glad. When we passed, a half-hour later, one brown leg and arm were as unconsumed as before. We were told that the frugal Hindus do not buy much wood, as it is dear, but half burn their dead, and then throw the remains in the Ganges.

ment, some eighty feet high, and solid rubble throughout, was most impressive as it stood there alone in the flat sunny landscape. Its grass-grown top and defated sides were different to when the Chinese pilgrims saw it in the seventh century, a miracle of gold and marble ; but the carved/designs still remain- ing round the sandstone base were admir- able, and most intricate, for the swastica was interlaced with another mazy geometrical pattern. There were also beautiful carved bands of marigolds. A museum is begun hard by,in which to assemble all the antiqui- ties that come to light from time to time in India. A great deal less than one expected remains of the olden Hindu times, at least in Northern India. But that one must put down to the repeated devastations which betel the land century after century, till the British raj brought security. Poor India! Her very wealth of fertility, as was the case with wealthy, fertile Italy, drew down upon her woes and ravages which poorer lands have never known. Over and over again the comparison between these two southern peninsulas of Europe and Asia suggested itself to us.

===== 0002601_19090724_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
A.-- GHASTLY SIGHT.

It was whilst we were rowing across to visit the palace of the Maharajah of Benares, further up the opposite bank, that my cousin remarked two crows come slowly

===== 0002601_19090724_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CENTRAL HINDU COLLEGE.

Before leaving Benares, we were deeply interested in visiting the Central Hindu College established by Mrs. Besant. Though this great work was only undertaken ten years ago, it has already resulted in a fine

spur of the wooded hills. Frequent Mists envelop it, and shut out the superb view of Kinchingunga, and his attendant mountains. We had mist the first day, and rain the second, but on the morning of the third, my energetic cousin roused me at five to come out and see the sun rise from the small plateau in front of the hotel. It was decidedly chilly as we paced about for half an hour in night- dress and overcoat, shawl and slippers, and the " pink glow" on Kinchingunga was of the pq,lest possible shade ; but to eastwards we saw an unparalleled sight. The whole tremendous valley, more than 4.000 feet deep, was filled half way up, with billowing clouds of vapour. They were motionless, but, strangest sight of all, had poured like a vast Niagara down one great hill side, following the steep slope all the way, and tossing like real billows over its edge. Yet quite suddenly, next to this cataract of grey, a hard line was drawn, and dark trees and tea-shrubs stood out, freed from any mist. We retired to warm, had " chota hazri " or small break- fast, a great institution for starting the day in India, and, walked round the Mall and Observatory- hill. It was just below this that the tremendous landslip of ten or twelve years ago took place. The ground is so steep that it has had to be banked up. We saw the printed prayer-rags of the Thibetans fluttering on bamboo poles on top of the little hill; but the mist rose and enveloped us, and the steep paths, in that rarified air, tried our breathing powers severely. The next day it rained. We visited the bazaar, and saw quantities of jolly-faced Thibetans, Bhotians, and Nepaulese. They are as ugly as they are dirty, having Mo.nsolian eyes and cheek- bones, lank black hair and leathery- skins, and certainly no acquaintance with soap! But their hearty spirits and keen sense of fun, and a certain swaggering, sturdy independence, recalling the characteristics of those _other sons of the mountain, the Tyrolese, give them a personality which appeals to one far more than that of the sleek untrustworthy Bengali. The Thibitans load themselves with turquoise ornaments set in silver. These they offer for sale saying earnestly, "Buy it miss sahib, buy it !" but laughing with the greatest good nature when one smiles contemptuously at the exhorbitant price, and tells them one is not going to be taken in. The next morning We had our reward for all this rain and mist. Kinchingunga was free of cloud and dazzlingly white. The profound gorges of the Rungeet and Teester were full 6f mazy blue and violet shadows; almost you could hear the rivers roaring at the foot of Kinchingunga's white mass, which • brooded mightily above them, and down below, nearly- 6,000 feet from where we stood, the profundity of the valleys was filled with blue morning haze. Behind us, the prairies of Bengal were unfor- tunately covered with sunny mist which

saved horse and rider from a roll of hundreds of feet.

Later on,when I went up to Sialkhot,Miss Moreland spent some days at Simla, and had an eventful ride along its snowy bridle paths. Returning, she met a drove of mules carrying wide packs which nearly filled up the bridle path. Her sais dismounted, but Miss More- land daringly rode past them, taking the outside o the path-. It was slippery with frozen snow, and on a sudden, she found her- self sitting on the edge of the cud whilst her pony's hind legs had gone down it! How she got off in time, she does not quite• remember, but the animal scrambled back safely, and next time she met a string of mules she kept to the inside of the path. • Here, however, her pony slipped, and they both plunged into a snow- bank and rolled in it whilst the mules went by! I parted from Miss Moreland, who was With some friends, at Darjeeling, and set off with Sam down the winding hill-railway, Heavy rain came on and blotted out the view, but I should not have appreciated it anyway, for the swirling curves, and the jolting of the little train, made one feel remarkably sick! No one was better suited to leave than Sam, who had been in a half- frozen and torpid state during our winter days on the mountains. He thawed as we neared the plains, and no wonder, for by the time we were on the river bank, awaiting a steamer for Eastern Bengal, the heat was intense. Our journey was long and most fatiguing. I was making for Chittagong, to visit Mr. and Mrs. Venters, and anyone who looks at the map will see what a cross- country . journey this meant. Even the station-masters, than whom there are no more obliging and helpful officials in India, were not sure of which steamer I should take, where I should change, and whether I should wait on a mail at the junction, which meant hours in the refreshment room, or go on by a slow. But at length, after two days and nights, I reached Chittagong and my friends.

===== 0002601_19090724_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DELHI GATE FORT.

floating down the river, evidently perched upon something. At the same time, a pariah dog came louping slowly over the sands, and a vulture, meditating on a spit of sand, hopped rreluctantly away at his approach. So well did the dog time matters, that he waded in at the point just as the crows and their prey reached it. "It is a drowned cow" said my cousin, !` I can see the brown and red splotches on its white hide." But it was not a cow. As the boat passed, we saw a chalk-white body, inflated by the water, and the dog gently drawing it to shore by the loin cloth. The face we mercifully could not see; but the memory of the drowned Cingalese at Mount Lavinia returned to me, and we both took a horror of the rivers and their fish!

college being built, with close-on a thousand students, airy classrooms, large exercise grounds, and the interested favour of many of the native princes to help it on. Ethics and philosophy on the ancient Hindu lines are taught here. Those who know the teaching of the Vedanta, as Max Muller did, declare that ,under a different guise they are as lofty and ennobling as our own Christian ones: and the Central Hindu College is seek- ing to elevate the rising generation on their own lines, instead of converting them to a creed against which they are prejudiced, because it has never belonged to them nor to their forefathers. We left Benares with regret, and travelled down, by the splendid Punjab Mail once more to Calcutta, whence a twenty-four hours' journey took us up to Darjeeling.

===== 0002601_19090724_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TEMPLES.

Benares city consists of narrow and tortuous lanes, mostly stone-paved and dripping with sacrificial oils and perfumed water, and the mangled remains of marigold wreaths. . The temples are numerous, of course, and amongst them is one with its conical roof entirely covered with gold leaf. The effect in sunshine as you view it from a balcony opposite, is dazzling. All day the sound of brazen gongs seems to go on The elephant head of Ganesh, and Shiva's bull, are sculptured everywhere. The rich and minute carving on lintels, door-posts, and roofs, is very fine. Christians may not of course enter the precincts; but outside Benares stands a monkey temple, where yisitors may feed the finny, grey-fated

===== 0002601_19090724_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
DARJEELING.

What a contrast to leave the hot damp plains, crowded with subservient Hindus— for alas no one can pretend to be much impressed by the Bengali—and to find oneself winding up hour by hour through dense forest growth, in a tiny train, which sometimes went head, sometimes tail fore- most, according as the line zigzagged and twisted. In one place the line makes a com- plete circle within eighty yards and comes back underneath .itself. It was not till we reached Ghoom, a village 7,400 feet up, that we caught our first sight of the everlasting snows on the highest mountains in the world.

Darjeeling is 7,000 feet up, lying on a steep

must have been to them their sky. That night we witnessed a Thibetan fancy-dress dance, on the plateau outside the hotel. These mountain-folk have a great idea of dressing up and acting. I suppose it comes in useful for amusement in the cold dark winter evenings. Anyway, four men made up into two lions or gigantic dogs, which' playfully fought each other and then retired' panting, while other weird animals filled in the time between the bouts. Never for- a minute did the lions (or dogs!) forget to pant, their great red tongues, to roll their eyes,and occasionally wag their tails or thump them on the ground. Then they would bend round and scratch their ears. MI this, seen by torchlight to the tumtumming of monotonousi instruments, and framed in with earnest,ugly faces was a sight to remember. I heard a wonderful account of THE FIRST VISIT OF THE DALAI LAMA, or rather his earthly representative, a dignified young man, who for the first time came from beyond the guarding frontier-walls of Thibet. As this young man and his suite had never see hotels, electric light, soda water,, hill-railways and so forth, some amusing incidents took place; though his admirable dignity prevented his showing astonishment openly. Before leaving Darjeeling, I must mention the tea. It is fit to be the drink of gods; never have I met with such tea before. We drank at least six times as much as was good for us, merely from delight in its flavour. The tea plantations clothe all available slopes with their glossy dark shrubs. These belong to the same genus as the camellia tree. It would have beerrcurious if we'had elected to say "Will you have some camellia" ? Also, one word more on the" cud," which is the hill-side below roads or tracks, and as often as not, almost a precipice.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 24, 1909.

CHITTAGONG.

Chittagong is the headquarters of the Bengal-Assam line, and is a large settlement three miles inland of the Bay of Bengal. Every bungalow is built on a small sandy hillock of its own, and the riding is delight- ful as the paths are so soft. It is not very far north of Rangoon, and warm even in winter time. In the summer, I hear, ants, mosquitos and insects generally, are nearly as bad as the heat, whilst snakes. lurk in all the compounds,' or gardens. But a breeze blows from the sea which mercifully tempers the heat. There is a beautiful airy club- house, and a. golf links and .tennis courts much used by the large and pleasant European colony ' • but as there is nothing specially flinduish in Chittagong I will hurry back through Calcutta and up to Cawnpore, where I again joined Miss

THE CUD.

My last morning, we rode out at six, and made down towards the wooded spur of Lebong,on which stand the summer quarters of several regiments (surely a heavenly spot for soldiers to be stationed !) We were followed by a small sais, or groom, on a very untractable Thibetan pony. It charged once madly between Miss Moreland and myself,at ' a gradient of perhaps one in six downwards. He recovered from this only to have a terrific bout of plunging and pulling a few moments later behind us. We turned round to see the pony dash recklessly sideways, throw the terrified sais up on the sky line, and then disappear with him! My cousin had thrown the reins to me, nipped off her animal and plunged through tea bushes in a moment. Mercifully, the cld here bulged outward, so that it admitted of tea bushes beiDg grown, and by one Of these my cousin found the sais lying uninjured but very frightened, though he had never let go of the pony's bridle, good fellQw ! Twenty yards further back, had the pony leapt sideways, nothing could have

Moreland, a4.whence we travelled on to Delhi.

DELHI AND THE SIEGE.

This celebrated city stands out in every English mind as corn nemorating one of the most daring, sieges in history. " All the passion, the tragedy, and the glory of the Indian Mutiny" says Fitchett," gather round those three sieges, Cawnpore, Luck- now and Delhi. But in the last named, it was the English themselves, the handeut of white men on the Ridge outside the city, who besieged the thousands and thousands of Sepoys within. _ _

. Lord- Roberts has admirably described that heroic siege in his" Forty-one years in India," for he himself was in it, and spoke with the great Nicholson, who died on the Ridge, thanking heaven he still had strength left to shoot the man who might waver and throw up the Beige. His statue now stands in gardens near his grave, facing towards the Kashmir gate where that last and victorious assault was carried out. It is invidious to mention names where nearly everyone was conspicuous for bravery. But surely we should all remind ourselves of that superman, John Nicholson, that example of unfaltering bravery and herculean endurance, whose personality towers through the Mutiny, and whose presence left its stamp on generations after ; and of Lieutenant Willoughby and Conductor Scully, who blew up the powder magazine after defending it with nine men only, against the raging city ; and of Salkpld and Corporal Burgess, who with others, blew up the Kashmir gate in the teeth of the enemy's fire. Nor can one omit mentioning Hodson, who for sheer cool daring and mastery of nerve can surely not be surpassed even In the world's history. But all this is in the pages of Fitchett, and I must hurry on to describe Delhi and catch the mail northwards. _

Of course, the Fortress-Palace, built by that great Mogul, Shah Jehan, the grandson of Akbar, claims first place. It is built of rose red sandstone, and its walls, a mile in extent, tower up sixty feet or so against the pale blue sky. Within, are the celebrated white marble palace apartments where the receptions at the Delhi nurbar took place. These exquisite rooms are open on all sides to let cool air play through; their windows are openwork marble screens, looking out over low green shrubs and grass to ,the Jumna. Down the centre of the suite of 'rooms runs a little channel, perhaps a foot in depth and width, along which, in the days when the Emperor's Court lived here, water rippled ceaselessly. Now it is quite dry. Crowning beauty of all is the Pearl Mosque, a small building entirely faced with white Marble, varied here and there with palest washes of green. The whole is sculptured in low relief with marigolds, irises, and beauti- ful designs unknown to us before; and th.o

effect is like the tratsparent foam curling on the tips of waves. Some way off stdnds the Jumma Musjid, or Big Mosqu6, built of rose sandstone with lines of white marble running down its minarets and walls. Big it certainly was, as we found on climbing one of the minarets, but I did not admire the colouring. Outside Delhi, the most wonderful build- ings star the plain on which seven other Delhis are said to have stood within historic times. The old for- tress palace is unforgettably beautiful, its huge crimson gateway reared its bell- shaped cupolas, still enamelled with vanish- ing blue and green tiles, a hundred feet above our heads, as we walked up the grass grown roadway. Inside,were silent and uninhabited palaces. Eleven miles out across the dusty plain towers the Kutab Minar, one of the veritable wonders of the world. It is, I think, 470 feet high, and sculptured from top to base, and was erected by the Mohammedan dynasty to celebrate their victory over the effete Hindus. We went up it—indeed, one of our chief forms of exercise was minaret- climbing, and it make you feel quite certain that unusual muscles have come into play; —and gazed over that dusty, waterless plain stretching to the hazy distance on all sides, whilst Delhi, afar off" sparkled like a grain of salt." Our carriage had certainly "toiled through immeasurable wastes of sand" that day, and a pleasant excursioh was to the huge solitary tomb of Akbar, where grassy- lawns and fragrant trees lay round the rose- stone mausoleum, and peacocks and monkeys played in solitary safety among the gardens. That evening we took the night mail to La here.

WOMEN'S GHAT.

Ancient Ttibutes and Set vices to the King.—When the King passes through Chichester next week, on his way from Goodwood House to the Solent, he could if he liked demand of the inhabitsnts a string for his crossbow. That is still one of many peculiar tributes and services, which, according to ancient custom the Sovereign could exact. At other places the King could ask for eels, fat geese, herring pies, or some similar delicacies, whilst several of the dukes hold their lands conditionally on the performance of some nominal form of rent quittance. The Munro of Foules for instance, have to present the Sovereign with a snow- ball, should the King honour them at any time with a visit, rt condition which would rake the tenure of their property somewhat precarious were it not that the snow-clap Ben Wyris is near by. Perhaps most, curious of all was the grant of certain lands near Dover to Solomon Attifeld in the thirteenth century, on condition" that sa often as the King should cross the sea, the said Solomon or his heirs, should he obliged to go with him to hold his tlajesty's head if he should be seasick." History is a little obscure as to when the last Royal head-holding tork place, but Hasted in his history of Kent says that the lands were held on the same terms down to the middle of the fifteenth Century. Paignfon's Mammoth Pudding.—The " Paignton Pud ling" festival proposed to be revive:l this year, is leferred to in Westcote's " View of Devonshire in 1630," and is of very ancient origin. No•doubt it was originally an annual village feast and revel; which, in course of time took piace only at longer interva's on occasions of national rejoicing, or to celebrate some important local event, and was finally made a jubilee festival. The last Pudding celebration to ik place in 1859 to commemorate the opening of the railway to London. The pudding consisted of 5731b5. of flour, 3821b5. of suet, 3821b5. of raisins, 1911bs. of currants, ' with sugar, milk, eggs, and spices in corresponding pro- portions and when cooked it weighed a ton and a half. The feast was laid out on Paignton Green on August Ist, but according to contemporary accounts, it ended in a scene of wild confusion. The display of good things attracted a huge mob of uninvited guests wl o proposed to help themselves, and this led to a pitched battle between the defenders and outsiders in which the mammoth pudding served piecemeal for missiles. Tea Parties for Working Girls.—Attention is called to the fact that country residents who have gardens and meadows, could give working girls a good deal of pleasure at little cost, by entertaining small parties of them to tea, and outdoor games in the summer time. An appeal has been made to country residents near London, who may be disposed to extend hospitality in Ith a way, and a coirmunication with Miss Nicholls, of the Women's Branch of the Social Institutes Union, 39, Princes-gate, S W., wobld at once put them in the way of carry ing it into effect. There is the same oppor- tunity for well-to-do re 'pie in tIo neighbourhood of all the large industrial towns. Most work girls are allowed a few days or a week's holiday by their employers, and those that cannot afford a visit to the country or the seaside—and they are the many—R ould be grateful to kind-heartect people willing to give them a day's pleasure, end an opportunity to enjoy the beauties of Nature.

On Being Affable.—Peihaps the railway companies will follow the Postmaster-General's example and ask their servants to extend to the public that "courts, good humour, and willingness to oblige "which Mr. Sydney Buxton says are so desirable in the manners of Post Office counter attendants. It, is, however, not so often that one meets with incivility from the "young lady " postal clerks, as from railway officials, and in both cases it is very frequently the stupidity of the public which provokes the rudeness. The lady who worried the Scots railway porter with a long rigmarole of silly questions, and was finally told to go to an undesirable place, affords an instance. For a long time the porter refused to apologise, but eventually going up to the lady, he said," Youpeedna " which he deemed a sufficient apology considering the aggravation he had received. Then again for some obscure reason, it seems to have been deemed unnecessary to put up the name of the station over the platforms at the London termini or principal railway stations. Hence passengers frequently ask if the train is going to the particular station they want, when they have already arrived there. This occurred the other day when a lady asked if her train was going to London Bridge—she being already there—" If you want London Bridge," said the official," you will probably and it outside at the end of that passage, anyhow it, was there this morning." He had probably been asked the same question scores of times that day, and evidently his fund of good humour was exhausted. Dangerous Hair-Washes.—The police in some of the smaller German principalities forbid women from wearing long skirts in the streets, nor must they wear boots with excessively high heels, or hatpins with dangerously protruding ends. In their anxiety to pre- vent dirt, disease, and disaster, the German police code sanctions many things which we in this country would regard as unwarrantable interference with oar personal liberty, but there are seine precautions with respect to which we might follow the example of our German friends, and one of them is the prohibition of the use of dangerous hair-washes. Attention is called to this danger by an inquest on a lady who died last week in London while undergoing a dry shampoo. The use of hydra-carbons for the purpose, highly volatile and inflammable preparations which have all the effect of chloroform, has led to many disasters, and clearly hair- dressers should be compelled to take greater precautions, if indeed they should be allowed to use suchiiieadly preparation!

- Changes in Hairdressing.—Hairdressers should be amassing huge fortunes, for modes are always changing, and "transformations" and other "additions" that answer well one season, ara hopelessly out of state the next. Just now, the small neat head is in vogue, decorated with a largo coil or thick plait, and as no amount of scheming and planning can transform the bunch of curls, lately worn, into a tail ayard long, hairdressers are undoubtedly again reaping a rich harvest. Commonsense in Fashions.—How is it, one wonders, that the merely middle-height or short woman invariably chooses the biggest hat, and the biggest ruffle she can find? Could she see herself as others see her, she would have more consideration, both for herself, and those who are forced to look at her. The ruffle, like the big hat, needs a tall, slender-necked figure to carry it off successfully, and for less fortunate individuals there is always the scarf. All the newest scarves, by the way, are finished with long silken fringes, vvhicb, beside being highly ornamental; are useful in affording the necessary weight to keep the ends from excessive floatinn. propensities. Bathing Attire.--Modes for every description of sea bathing are available this year. For the" dry bather " who saunters up and down on the brink of the waves, there are the Princess and Renaissance 2e gowns " built of heavy satin and worn over carefully cut corsets made of corded twill fitted*with suspenders to hold the stockings in place. Costly hats are a part of the " dry bathers ' outfit, also dainty hosiery, sandals with twine soles, and a tall cane._For the real bather the blouse and short skirt of blue serge ranks high in favour, together with a very simple hat or handkerchief turban while the swimmer can find the useful and desirable stockingette garment, in which to enjoy, unfettered, her time in the ocean.

THE CHAIRMAN AND VIE LAND MONOPOLY. The CHAIRMAN, after extending a hearty welcome to the sptekers, said we were living in stirring times. The last remains of the feudal system were breaking up and a new order of society was slowly emerging. There were some people who did not like the change. The Duke of Rutland, for instance, would like to put a gag in the mouth of every Labour member and keep it there. (Laughter). But the Duke of Rutland might gag the lot, and others would rise and take their places. (Applause). He could not , stop the onward march of the progressive forces. Another man had descended to use a meaner and more contemptible weapon than the gag—slander: Be (the speaker) was sure everyone , present would rejoice at the splendid vindication of Mr. Will Thorne—(applause)-4rom the slander of Earl Winterton—who would not forget as long as he lived the lesson he bad been taught. The Budget, it had been said, marked an epoch in the history of revenue. It did far more than that. It marked an epoch in the social history of this couutry—(applause)—for the social and economic effects of the Budget would far transcend any financial gain. The opposition and the support of the Budget arose from entirely different social ou'looks. On one hand they bad vast landed interests, inheritors of great wealth, and these people held in their own hands the very lives and destinies of their fellow men. On the other side they had people who believed that this vast accumulation of land and wealth was a positive danger, ayea real menace to the welfare of the State. (Hear, hear). The Tories said "Tax bread" but the Liberals said "No, tax land." The landlord said " Tax everything brit me" but the Liberals said" The necessities of life shall be free." (Applause.) The present land system was a block in the way of social reform. There was no question for which the plea of urgency could be advanced with greater force. The monopoly in land allowed in this country was unjust and oppressive. Could it be right that thousands and thousands of acres of land should be shut up from us-,, and kept as parks, pleasure grounds and game preserves, when four-fifths of the people were housed on 600,000 acres out of 77,900,000 acres? ("No.") It was time this growing, greedy monopoly should be broken once and for ever. It was said that the Govern- ment would not raise much in the first year through the land clanses of the Budget. He was thankful to say they had got the wedge between the door and the portals and they would be able to drive it home just as the needs of the State required. He believed the people had made up their minds that the land clauses must be maintained and that there must be no weakening on that point. They were awakening to the fact that, in the words of Cobden, they had been " cheated, robbed and bamboozled" long enough, and they were not going to stand it any longer. It was said the House of Lords were going to reject the Budget. If they did it was " good-bye " to the House. of Lords. (Applause.) He thought the Lords had more sense than to do that. Lord Lansdowne let the people down over the Licensing Bill, but be was not going .to do EO with the Budget. They were going to see it through in its entirety. The Lords defeated them in the great effort to deal with the gigantic drink evil, but that defeat was only victory poatponed. The pro- gressive forces of this country would yet triumph (Applause.)

BACKING THE BUDGET.

RECORD OPEN-AIR MEETING AT WARRINGTON.

===== 0002601_19090724_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
Sp3eches by Messrs. A. H. Crosfield. M.P., A. G. C. Harvey, M.P ,

and ethers.

One of the largest if not the largest open-air political meeting ever held in Warrington was the demonstration organized by the Warrington Pro- gressive Labour League in support of the Budget, which attracted a huge crowd to Bridge Foot (near Arpley Cannons) on Monday evening. The great enthusiasm which characterised the proceqdings showed how Mr. Lloyd-George's courageous and far- reaching Budget proposals commend themselves to the workers, and a notable feature was the entire absence of hostile interniptions, the speeches being followed with the closest attention. Mr. E.Wynue Jones (president of the League) presided and was supported on the platform by Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., Mr. A. G. C. Harvey, M.P. (Rochdale), the Rev. C. Harvey- Cook, Councillors Locker and Skelland, Messrs. C. E. Parker, J. Dodds, E. England, J. Elwell,' S. Wright; J. Howes, jun., and J. Morton (secretaries of the League.)

it was their bounden duty, bsving regard to every con- sideration of English manliness and chivalry, to con- sider the needs of women sad also of the children, he would not merely say on a level with the needs of men for be believed they ought to give them the preference. He was glad therefore to hear the approval with which his suggestion was received—that it would be

longed debates, I really do not think I have ever seen such a combination of cheeriness, patience and courage to opponents, with untlinchirg and unswerving courage and ability, as the Chancellor himself displays. (Applause). Therefore, not alone because the Bridget is a splendid thing in itself but even for personal reasons, I ask you to allow me to take back to Westminster a message of very hearty and determined suppoit both to Ale Prime Minkter and to his gifted, brilliant and indomitable Chancellor." (Loud applause). -

MR. HARVEY EXLPAIS THE BUDGET. THE LORDS' ATTITUDE.

Mr. HARVEY, who was also enthusiastically greeted, said that on any occasion he would be highly gratified to stand on a political platform with Mr. Crosfield, to show how highly his colleagues in the House of Commocs appreciated their member's influence and power far good. (Applause.) He was rarticularly glad to he on a Progressive platform at the present time because they, had come to a crisis in their history. They were faced with a battle and they faced it unsh'inkingly and unafraid, in fact it was the very qght they had been wanting for many years back. (Hear, hear.) Now the day had come they would buckle on their armour and go into the battle with the conscious- ness that right and justice were with them and that the day of democracy was now to dawn.(Applause.)Referring to, the accusations of extravagance made against the , Government, Mr. Harvey said that for a Tory to talk alma extravagance was like Satan renroving sin. (Laughter.) If there were any Tories in Warrington - (laughter)—and they happened to be present, be would like to remind theba of one or two financial facts. When the Liberals went out of power in 1895 they left the expenditure of the country at 102 millions per annum. When the Tories went out of power in 1905 they bad taken it up to 162 millions per annum. They had also given us a war so ill-managed and so ill-calcu- lated that with the actual cost of carrying it out and the increased expenditure it had since entailed upon us, he could prove that they had squandered 1,000 millions of the people's money. The Liberals had had 31 years of government, and what had they done in tivit time? They had led uced the debt of the country by millions and millions of money. They had even done a better thing than that—they had put the finances of \the ' country on a SOUND AND BUSINESSLIKE BASIS.

Catholic schools. He (Mr. Harvey) did not object to the Catholic schools being upheld; Catholics had long and properly, according to their consciences, defended their schools. But the Duke of Norfolk was doing all this in the sacred cause of religion. He (Mr. Harvey) ventured to be a bit sceptical on the point. He thought the Duke was more afraid of land reform than of any danger to the Catholic schools. A short time ago the Derwent valley wanted water, and what was of greater necess:ty to the health and well-being of a community? Per the purposes of the collecting-ground or reservoirs some 500 acres owned by' the Duke of Norfolk were required. The value of the land before the public wanted it was £IO,OOO but the public had to pay his Grace nearly £70,00. (" Shame.") Mr. Harvey showed how the land question lay at the root of all progress and social reform, and he observed that the Budget enshrined a policy which would do the greatest thing of all—secure a valuation of the land. Before they could get serious and substantial land reform, they must have a valuation of every acre of land in the country. (Applause.) Supposing is man, anxious to escape the development tax, put his land value very low, his increment tax would he very high, and if he put a high value on it in order some day, to escape his increment tax, his develop- ment tax would Le very high, so that the poor fellow could do nothing except Le just and true with the authorities in all his dealings, and present a correct and honest valuation. (Applause.) Mr. Harvey showed how our land system handicapped our progress as com- pared with other countries. It was very strange to see, when one went to Germany, how well the people thrived under those conditions of Protection which we ',new were bad for them, and which we would not have at any price. One could go to towns in Germany where they had everything that apparently the public could desire and the rtes were very low—in fact he had been in towns in Germany where rates did not exist at all. Why ? Because the municipality owned sufflcient land and had developed it,so that it paid the a hole of the expenses of governing the town. (Applause). The men who were opposing the Government would not only withhold the land but they would give us Protection, and of late we had seen that they would give us conscription and compulsory service on the top of it. They would give us a tariff on imports because they were so used through their land ownership to put tariffs on trade and the people's natural development. T hey would give its compulsory service because they were used and accustomed to see people controlled and held up, confined in their develop- ment and in the increase of their powers by Leing more or less serfs on the soil which these nien owned nni controlled and were lords and kings of. The batt!e must go forward; there would he no turning back. Lord Landsdowne threatened the Government with muffled thunder. The bright and sharp lightning of Winston Churchill shot from the neighbouring hill. Mance met defloance. It would soon, perchance, be for the people to dec'de. lie did not believe their lordships would be so mad, so lost to all sense of propriety and reasonableness, as to refect the Budget but if they did the weieht of responsibility would be on the people ; it would be the testing time of democracy. They had the strength of the Progressiv* forces in the House of Commons in the two parties—Liberal and Labour— marching side by side, shoulder to shoulder, with one object and aim. When they came to the people and asked them to back them up in the efforts they had made, he trusted—in fact he was sure—they would give the Government their approval and confidence. (Loud applause).
