MR. OROSFIELD'S SPEECH.

A FAIR PRINCIPLE.

(Hear, hear.) He had read a pretty ir*riant speech delivered last week by a very influential man in_the House of Lords—Lord. Lansdowne—who objected to the taxes being put on beer and tobacco, because,he said,t hey had had no business to take them off tea and sugar. There they came to a very straight issue. Were they to go back on that proposal and instead of taxing what were, after all, agreeable luxuries if they liked, but will in a sense and comparatively speaking luxuries—beer and tobacco—and tax tea? (Cries of "No.") In that con- nection he would put one consideration before them. They knew that at the present time while women had a great deal of influence in local politics, and he must say in his opinion a very great and proper influence in Imperial politics too; they had not, for the purposes of the Imperial Parliament, got the vote. A lady friend of his who was taking a very active part in the movement for femsle suffrage gave him a tip very frankly the other day—(laughter)—and he did not mind telling them what it was. (Laughter). She said "You may look out in your constituency for a lively bit of heckling presently." (Laughter). It was quite true that all of them at Westminster now had got to mind their p's and q's a bit. They vrera generally making their way home any time between two and three in the morning, and he could assure them that even at that hour—and he said it with the most sincere admiration for their zeal—he had seen some or their lady friends who were bent on the suffrage way-laying the unfortu- nate Prime Minister at the gates. (Laughter). And so, since his lady friend gave him that tip, he (Mr. Crosfleld) had pictured himself leaving the House and having a look at the Palace gates to see it the watch- ful suffragists were there, making a dash for the under- ground railway or, if they were guarding the station, trying surreptitiously to make his way to a hansom and get home. (Laughter). Speaking quite seriously, .they must frankly admit that there was a great division of opinion in their party on

THE SUFFRAGE QUESTION. He had always realized that the time might come when they would see in this country adult suffrage adopted without the exclusion of either sex; and everyone of age might then have the vote. That might be advisable, but be would say candidly that he would not like to see adult suffrage for the whole population carriel now, because he could not but think that, as the avenues of admirable, useful and most important work were now opened to all their lady friends, if adult suffrage were eventually to be granted, it would not be a bid thing for them to have ten or twenty years of training in local government before'taking what was not only a privilege but a great responsibility in the matter of Imperial government. He might be wrong in holding those views but what he wished to emphasise was that those who thought that way above all must realise that

ACHIEVEMENTS OF THE GOVERNMENT Mr. Crosfield slid it was safe to say they bad got. through more work than any democratic Parliament that ever sat in this country. To recapitulate a few of the leading measures, they had first of all the extension of the Workmen's Compensation Act. Then he mould remind them of what they discussed at considerable length before the election—the Trades Disputes Bill. As bearing upon the important question of the physique of the nation end to the attention the Government was giving to the young, there was the splendid piece of work Mr. llerbertSamuel carried through in the Bill for the Medical Inspection of Children. Let them consider what had been done for the feeding of tho• e poor little ones that went to school underfed; and also another humane subject they had dealt with—the reform of our prison*. Of all that the Government bad done, he thought cne might say with Mr. Birrell tint probably no measure had been pissed by Parliament for genera- tions which had brought about a greater increase in the sum of human happiness than the Old Age Pensions Act. (Loud applause). Mr. Crosfield allusled to the importance of the Budget in view of the prospective extension of the provisions of this Act. He believed the Budget would be carried through for they at West- minster wet() convinced that in supporting it they were not merely going in for progressive finance; they were working for a cause which was really the cause of the pe,ple of this coun'ry and which had real moral as well as polit'cal force behind it. (Applause). Con, vinced of alit, they would if necessary sit until Christmas in order to carry the Budget, aid it was only in the constituencies that they waned a determined and enthusiastic backing for Mr. Lloyd-Gearge W:10 was one of the finest champions of the democracy the country had ever had. (Applause). Tha House of Commons had also been d.mling with Mr. Winston Churchill's proposals for Labour Exchanges, and he (Mr. Crosfield) was sure that a great industrial con- stituency like Warrington must feel a very reit con- x ic'ion of the groat Importance and blessing to our country that such a system would be. Be could not think of anything more heart-breaking and miserable than for a man to be tramping about the country, very likely in the wrong direction, in search of employment, but in vain, when by the necessary organization he might be brought into touch" with an employer who bad a situation to offer hid). He believed also his con- stituents would approve of the steps which had been taken—and he bad the honour of serving on the com- mittee—with a view to the establishment of Trade Boards to get rid of the detestable system of sweating which everyone of them hated. (Applause). If the Budget went through, It would enable them to do more in the work for s )ctal and industrial reform. They would be able to institute a system of mutual .

INSURANCE AGAINST UNEMPLOYMENT which to his mind was a matter of absolutely the first importance. Nor did be think it was less necessary that they should carry through a scheme of insurance that would also cover invalidity and one of the saelckst things in life—premature breakdown in middle age. Besides furthering sach reforms, the Budget would gibe them spare cash for the development grant to encourage the agricultural industry in which he was snre they took a deep interest, knowing how essential it was for the well-being of tbe nation that a consider- able proportion of our people should be kept on the land and, as fur as possible, get back to the land of old England. (Applause). They had perhaps heard it said that the Budget was really a battle between rich and poor. He was glad to say frankly that, for the credit of our English public life, it was not quite so bad as that. He was thankful that there were not only middle-class people and capitalists--and he sas a little bit of a capitalist in a small way himself— (laughter)—amongst the Chancellor's Most determined backers, but slveral landed proprietors were as heartily supporting the Budget as anyone present at that meet- ing. That was the kind of thing that made one proud of the whole tone of English political iife and he hoped they would not forget that the gc,od tone and the comparative, in fact he would say the entire purity of English public life was enormously due to the great battle for Free rrade and purity in politics which their forefathers won for them. (Applause). Not only had they landowners and capitalists backing the Budget, hut he was glad to say that some of the Pters—they said many herd things about Peers and with justice—were public-spirited also. Some little time since he told h's friend

LORD ACTON that thcy would be very glad if he would come down and address a meeting for them in Warrington. He (Mr. Crosfield) felt sure they would welcome him, not ..nly because he was the son of the life-long friend of their old chief Mr. Gladstone but b3caasa then were near and dear ties that associated Lord Acton with their own neighbourhood. (Applause). He beard that Lord Acton might be able.to come to that.meeting, but his lordship had wired him that he had got very important and pressing business to attend to which prevented his being with 'them. He added "Much gratified by kind message. Should have gladly supported you in defending Budget, since my sense of public honour precludes me from confounding my principles with my pocket." (Applause). Mr. Cr3sfield observed that that was a very worthy sentiment to recommend to some of Lord Acton's friends in the gilded chamber. Another r6ason why he asked the meeting to support the Budget was that; in addition to the splendid princrples and the democratic progress which it stood for, they had in Mr. Lloyd-George a man who was Leing facei not by the public spirited of the more fortunate of the comminity but by opposition brought by those who perhaps found that great possessions were a little too much for them and who did not re nember that, fortunate as they were, they should bear in mind the partnership in the national work which they owed to the people of their country. The opposition which Mr. Lloyd-Geoige was confronted with was relentless and carried to the length ot'personal bitterness. "These peop!e," continued Mr. Crosfield, "the masters of many millions, face the gallant little Welsh chieftain—(applause)—and upon my word some- times I remember that delightful old story about another David—many years befdre our David—who was going to be crushed by a mighty giant. But David was too much for the giant, and Lloyd-George, with such a backing as he is getting from the country, will not be crashei by the forces which are brought against him. (Applause).- I have seen men in my lifetime put in tight places, fighting a stiff battle with cheeriness and patience. I have seen men who could combine courage with perfect courtesy to opponents. But sitting behind the Chancellor night after nights through these pro.

Instead of buying things that they wanted immediately and paying for them out of loans, they had paid their way like honest men as they went along. (Applause). They had reduced taxation upon some of the prime necessities of life—tea and sugar. They had also rsduced the burden of the income tax on the small income tax payer, on whom, of course, it fell more heavily than on the man of greater wealth. On these matters they met with the furious anger of Lord Lans- downe who said the Government had been throwing away revenue. SO they had and he (Mr. Harvey) did not regret it. They had thrown away half the sugar tax, but they had thrown it lack agrin into tLe lap of the house wife and if they got more money from the Budget they would throw the other half back. (Applauss). The result of the expenditure was that this year the Chancellor of the Exchequer wanted some 14 millions of money extra. He bad cone forward courageous'y, trusting to the honour of British manhood and asking everyboiy to pay a little towards liquidating the amount, lie had tried in the fairest possible manner to fulfil that principle which Was one of the primary principles of Liberal finance—to apportion the burden according to the strength and capacity of the shoulde.s to carry it. What was the money reqoired for? £7,000,000 for old age pensions. There was notsa respectable citizen in the town who was not glad to pay for the pensions— (bear, hear)—and if the Budget surplus that they expected came to hand, he ventured to pred;ct that. one of the first things for which they would use that accumulated money would be to nund off the scheme and to speedily give to those poor old folks who had struggled almost to the bitter end and at last had to disqualify themselves by receiving poor relief, the pension which perhaps of all in some cases they most deserved. (Applause.) £3,000,000 was requ:red for the navy, and £1,000,000 for labour exchanges, the development grant and improvement of the roads. The remaining £3,000,000 was wanted because the people were not pouring into the Exchequer as .they used to do, a sum of money from a certain source which was always a very profitable source but he was not sure that it redounded to the credit of the country. Mr. Lloyd-George was going to raise the £14,000,000 frirly and justly from the luxuries of people who could afford it and he was not going to touch the necessities of those who could not. ['he income tax and' death duties would provide £7,000,000. That amount would be paid by the rich, by those who had the best things of this life, and it was tcsbe paid in such a way that in the various grades of riches no man would be more hardly hit than another. £6,000,000 would come from liquor licences, toacco tax &c.. end that was the part of the tax which every- body paid. Therefore they had £7,000,000 which the rich would exclusively pay and £6,000,000 which he rich and poor alike would pay according to their Wm- snruption of the articles which produced the revenue. Was that oppressive? (" No.") Then they came to the

There was growing up a vigorous social conscience which was making us feel in a deeper and more significant sense that we belonged to each other and that what we had to do was to work not simply for the advantap of our own outlook and prospects but for the advantage of every brother and sister on God's earth. A TAX ON THE WHOLE CUPBOARD.

He had alluded to the unearned increment and graduated taxation as grounds upon which the Budget should receive their support, but to his mind the strongest reason of all why they should give Mr. Lloyd-George a solid backing was that those who were trying to beat him down by one of the most serious attacl4s on democracy that had been made probably during the life-time of anybody present, were deliber- ately committed to an attack upon the whole house- hold cupheard. (Applause.) They were committed to tax butter, cheese and dairy produce; they were committe to tax beef and mutton, and they even went the length of proposing to tax bread itself. In these days we beard a great deal about the national physique. Some people in very high places had much to say on that subject and we were also told on great authority that we could not expect to rear an Imperial race in rookeries. That was very true, but he thought it would be as well if those who were so fond of reminding us of the facts would bear them in mind, insteld of shrieking out w hen the Chancellor proposed to put a halfpenny ti x upon undeveloped land. Ho (Mr. Harvey) said in answer that no land was private property in the fullest sense of the term. PATRIOTISM OR COWARDICE. Sir Jahn Brunner's Plain Speaking. The Budget proposals received the emphatic support of a great public meeting at Macclesfield on Wednesday night. A COLLIER'S INJURY.

Compensation Claim at Warrington.

At the Warrington County Court, on Thursday, before Deputy-Judge W. H. S. Oniton, Albert Naylor, collier, Tanhoose-lane, Burtonwood, brought a claim under the Workmen's Compensation Act for compensation for injuries received whilst following his employment, the respondents being the Collins Green Colliery Ca., Earlestown.s Mr. W. J. Lies (instructed by Mr. W. Hutchen, St. Helens) appeared for the applicant, and Mr. E. Peace, Wigan, for the respondents. Mr. Lias stated that Naylor, wtio had been in the employ of the respondents for 20- years, finished his work at four o'clock on the morning of January 28th He was going up the main brow to the pit eye, followed by a man named Cunliffe, when he came across two sets of waggons, full and empty. He had to gt between them to get to the pit eye, and he WPS making his way between the waggons when they were suddenly set in motion. Applicant was dragged along and crushed between a full waggon and the bar which supported the roof of the pit He was in the Cottage Hospital for 19 weeks, the tissues of his back being broken, the ribs fractured, and he also sustained internal injuries. He was not yetable to work.

„ Applicanton answer to Mr. Peace, admitted that there was a rule to the effect that no person must travel the haulage brow when the rope which worked the waggons was in motion. The rope was not in motion when be started to walk along the brow, He did nptassent to Mr. Peace's suggestion that the rule meant that the men could only travel the haulage brow during certain periods when the engine was not working and there was no danger of the rope starting. Dr. Lath uu said it would be a considerable perild before applicant recovered from his injuries. Robert Cunliffe, 72, Fairclough•street, Burtonwood, who was working with the applicant, corroborated his version of the accident and denied that applicant was riding on one of the waggons. For the defence Mr. Peace contended that applicant ohad no right to walk along the haulage brow until a signal\ had been givtn that the rope was not working and further that he must have been riding on one of the waggons, and was thus guilty of serious and wilful misconduct. It was impossible for the accident to have happened in the way suggested by the applicant, for tests had been made and showed that a man might stand between the waggons without being injured. After hearing evidence, his Honour gave an award in favour of the applicant, with an allowance of lls. weekly, the amount agreed upon, and costs on scale B

Education.

To be Let.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JULY 24TH, 1909.

There are some pleasant 'THE COMING intervals in the almost MEN. per pe tual strife of politics. One of these was provided by the dinner given to some junior members of the Ministry by the National Liberal Club on Wednesday night. It is scarcely necessary to remind our.readers that there have recently been several promotions in the Government. Mr. HERBERT SAMUEL has gone from the Home Office to the-Chan- cellorship of the Duchy of Lancaster; Mr. MASTERMAN has succeeded Mr. SAMUEL at the Home Office ; the MASTER of EMBANK has advanced from the position of Junior Whip to tha Under-Secretaryship for India; and Mr. HERBERT LEWIS, trained into local govern- ment as chairman of one of the Welsh County Councils, has become Parliamentary Secre- tary of the Local GovernMent Board. These are the men to whom the National Liberal Club paid spacial honour on Wednesday,and each of them appears to have made most inter- esting speeches, expressing above everything else the new spirit in politics—that considera- tion which places the welfare of the people first and foremost. The difficulty about the older kind of politician, as Mr. MASTERMA said, is that he is living in the new world and still thinking that he is living in the old. The entertainment of the junior members of the Government. by a great political organisation like the National Liberal Club is a wholly interesting innova- tion. It signifies that a sharp outlook is being kept for the coming men who are to be the future-rulers of the country. In what drrection do their thoughts trend? What is the measure of their ability? These are the questions that the National Liberal Club seems to have been asking itself.

The present condition of things provokes such inquiries. Most Ministries within living memory have been Made up of what irreverent persons would describe as "old stagers." Mr. GLADSTONE'S great difficulty froni the beginning to the end of his career was to find a place for any man of promise. He was surrounded by old Parliamentarians who could not be set aside. There was always a horde of Peers Of experience who expected high places in the Governent. The Disraeli and Gladstone and Salisbury Ministries 'were mainly Ministries made out of more or less titled men. The same sort of thing went on (though to a slightly diminished extent) until Sir HENRY cAMPBELL-BANNERMAN became the head of the Government. Then on the death of that remarkable Statesman, who was really the first Radical Prime Minister, Mr. ASQUITH came into power, He was young, as Prime Ministers go, and desirous of surrounding himself—without any neglect of the veterans of Statesmanship—with the men of the new time.

• If the facts were looked up, and the neces- sary comparisons were made, we should find, we imagine, that the present Ministry is, taken altogether, the youngest Ministry that has held office since the days, at any rate, of the younger PITT. It takes half a lifetime nowadays to rise to any sort of eminence in the House of Commons,unless there is a Prime Minister who has a faculty for the discovery of talent. This, however, is a faculty that Mr. ASQUITH most evidently possesses. It might be plausibly contended, indeed, that the strength of his Ministry is .in his young men. Speculation naturally centres itself on what they may become. Who, amongst all of them, is to be the next Prime Minister? The question is one that we shall not presume to answer. There are at least four possible Prime Ministers on the Government front bench, which is surely a remarkable satire on the Tory contention during Mr. BALFOUR'S premiership that no possible, no every moderately respectable, Government could be formed out of the Opposition. There js not more than one possible Prime Minister on the Opposition benches at present, and he is the man *who led the Tory party to overwhelming defeat. The youth and energy and 'strong incentive are all at present on the side of Liberalism. This is one of the great facts of political. history, and it was accentuated in a very interesting manner by the dinner at the National Liberal Club.

It's a long lane that THE ROAD TO has no turning. For THE BENCH. longer than most of us

care to remember there has been a growing feeling against a corres- pondingly growing tendency to link the magisterial bench to party politics. When the present Government came into office the Lord Chancellor foUnd(as he had just told the Peers) that the benches in the country, in the counties especially, were crowded with Unionists. The natural result of this.(" no , men of spirit would submit to it, "said he) was that the other side clamoured to him to appoint practically nobody but Liberals, so as to restore the balance. But had the balance been restored it would probably have soon gone wrong again. For it has become a recognised thing for men whose ambition centres upon the letters " J.P." to work through their party agent upon their party Member, and obtain as the reward of menacing pressure what ought only to be obtained at the reward of intelligence and integrity. It may be granted that the num- ber of fit men_in the two great parties is approximately equal, and that, under ideal conditions, an allotment of an equal nmnber of seats to each would probably work out well. But those ideal conditions are only hypothetically attainable. While party jobbery has to be made use of, very many men of eminent capabilities will refuse to take any steps at all to get themselve put upon the bench. What has been wrong, of course, in the past is'what Lord Loreburn mentioned: the Lord Chancellor has had to appoint thou- sands of magistrates per annum from localities in which he probably knew per- sonally not a single man. There has been no machinery of recommendation save the party one: and if the Chancellor hAs to choose between A, whose M.P. recommends him, and B, whose name is put forward from another source, knowing nothing about the

real qualification of either, he is.just as likely to be right in appointing the former as the latter. It is impo*ble, of course, to forecast the recommendations of the Royal Com- mission which is to consider the matter; but it may very well take the form of local impartial advisory committees to assist the Lords-Lieutenant and the Lord Chancellor.

• Only a month or two ELECTIONS AND back there were indi- THE BUDGET. cations of something like a slump in Govern- ment _stock. That was before the people understood the Budget, and when they were misled (some of them) into thinking it was a revolutionary measure. Time and under- standing have brought enlightenment and to-day the Budget stands in the position of first favourite among the legislative proposals of a generation. There has been quite a miniature general election during the last fortnight, and four contests have each and all resulted in signal triumphs for the Government. Mr. Samuel has retained his seat in the Cleveland Division of Derbyshire, and Mr. Gulland has held his in the Dumfries Burghs, whilst Mr. Hancock defeated his Conserva- tive opponent in Mid-Derby by an extra large majority owing to the hearty co-operation of the forces of Liberalism and Labour. The keenest fight of the lot was that in the High Peak Division of Derby- shire which resulted yesterday in a victory for the sitting member, Mr. Partington, by 347 votes, and incidentally in the humiliation of his opponents. It is no secret that the Trade and the landowning class made a great effort to give Mr. Lloyd George a slap through the overthrow of his newest Junior Lord of the Treasury. They have left no stone unturned, and no mud-puddle unstirred in order to weaken the Liberal position, staked as it was on the Budget. But Mr. Partington has triumphed unmis- takably. His majority may be only half what it was when he was carried in on the Free Trade wave in 1906, but his poll is actually greater. Moreover, it has to be remembered that every election in the division between 1885 and 1900 proclaimed High Peak a Tory seat. The campaign against the Budget has signally .failed. People—the great democracy—have at last realised that Liberal legislation aim at equalising the national burden and placing the heaviest load of taxation on the broad- est shoulders. The masses and the classes and the taxes are being adjusted to their equitable relative -proportion.

In his first report to

UNHEALTHY the Education SCHOOLS AND THt Authority on School REMEDY. Hygiene Dr. Hibbert, _ _ _ _ _

the Medical Officer, illustrates very forcibly the backward posi-. tion of the borough in respect to education equipment from the health point of view. Those who know anything of the schools in the borough will recognise that the strictures of the Medical Officer are by no means exaggerated,and this raises the doubt whether the borough has really benefited by its past policy of running education on the cheap. It will take a long time, and be a costly process, before we have the children of the. town in schools which will conduce to health and well-being, as well as mental development. We readily acknow- ledge that it is impossible all at once to bring about such a desirable consummation, but what does impress us in the report is that many of the faults can be remedied with comparative ease and with little expense. Dr. Hibbert tells us that the school buildings require inspection and appropriate treatment quite as urgently as the children, attending. In the matter of ventilation ,he says that the most frequent and marked defects are found to exist; that it is an excep- tion to enter a schoolroom which has been occupied for any length of time by the scholars to find the air at all fresh; that the windows are often the only means of ventila- tion and were in several cases found shut; and the ventilating tubes closed. It is pointed out that in many cases only a small part of each window can be made to open. We would commend the following suggestive paragraph of the report to all concerned in education:

" Without adequate ventilation of the school- rooms, the general health of teachers and scholars deteriorates, while infectious disease finds the most favourable conditions for its spread. Colds' are often attributed to draughts, but— and I would especially submit this point to the teathers themselves—, colds' are not so much caused by fresh air admitted from without as due to the polluted atmosphere of the ill-ventilated interior. Not only from the point of view of the child's physical welfare is fresh air a constant necessity, but it is also impossible for a child to derive full benefit from the proffered teaching when the brain is dulled with the poisons of a stuffy atmosphere."

When we come to the question of light, the structural difficulties are considerably greater, and in view of surrounding buildings blocking out the light of various schools it will be impossible to improve them in this particular, and the only cure is to push for- ward the building of modern well-lighted schools. Dr. Hibbert tells us that " many- of the schools are by no means recent struc- tures, and do not comply with the modern, ideas on school construction. In several instances the buildings were not, I believe, 'originally intended as schools; and even in the schools built as such, the requirements for parochial purposes were chiefly considered, and , the sanitary defects characteristic of the parent building were often copied." The doctor strongly condemns the sanitary conveniences of many schools, particularly those where the repulsive and unhealthy pail system is in force. Unfortunately the whole town suffers in a similar way through inadequate sewers, and remedy is only possible by a comprehensive scheme which will bring the sewage system up to the required standard. In regard to the chil- dre,n themselves, 968 have been medically inspected, and tables are given with full particulars, of which the following are the most important: Verminous condition of head, 226 per cent.; defective eyesight (errors of refraction), 141) per cent. ; defective teeth, 223 per cent..; enlarged tonsils, 9'o per cent. Altogether, there is reason to be grateful to the Medical Officer for taking such pains to open the eyes of those in authority in regard to nuisances and drawbacks affect- ing the children in our schools which, while not exactly discoveries, have been neglected because of that familiarity which Often leads to contempt.

Here -is a story of • a FREEDOM AND fight for freedom in a FREE BEER. Surrey township. At Reigate, on July Ist, 1909, all the public-houses except five raised the price of their beer by the amount agreed upon among the brewers. Ten years ago in that part of England that decision would have been accepted as part of the workings of the universe. But since then there has been a change of spirit in Reigate, as well as a change of Parliamentary representation. As soon as the price had, been raised— by the very firms which had been asking for" eight more Dreadnoughts " and refusing to wait—the labourers ceased to drink beer

at those houses. They sent for ginger-beer. What beer they bought they took from the five other houses. What was the result? Within twenty-four hours those publicans found themselves •idle men. They just fell into the ranks of the unemployed. Within another twenty-four hours they were sending &deputation to the brewers to allow them to lower their prices. Within another twelve. hours the price.was reduced. And now they are giving beer away in the vain hope of recapturing the lost trade

The Warrington Pro- WARRINGTON AND gressive Labour . THE BUDGET. League has every reason to be proud of, the part it has taken in the Budget campaign. There have been many large and enthusiastic open-air meetings in support of the progres- sive cause of late years, but Monday even- ing's demonstration in defence of the Budget was probably a record. The crowd could not lie,ve been far short of 3,000, and there was not a single dissentient note—in fact it was evident that the meeting was determined and enthusiastic in its support of Mr. Lloyd- George's democratic and far-reaching -proposals. Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., received an enthusiastic welcome and the convincing and direct way in which he stated the case for the Budget and the Government's other admirable measures of reform, held the close attention of the audience. The telegram from Lord Acton succinctly stating his reason for supporting the Budget, was received with gratification, his lordship's public-spirited view of the measure being in striking contrast to the selfish attitude of some noble lords who are fuming and threatening. Mr. A. G. C. Harvey, M.P., delivered a very powerful and eloquent speech, and carried his hearers with him in his lucid exposition and wholehearted support of the Budget. Mr. Harvey himself was deeply impressed with the meeting, for, writing from the Reform Club, London, to Mr. James Dodds (whose guest he was) he says ".1 have told many of my friends here about the meeting, which was one of the most encouraging I have ever attended and whiclt filled me with hope for the future."

‘OTES AND NOTIONS.

The Budget does not fall only on the rich. It is just all round. It taxes the luxuries of all classes, while the Tariff Reformers want to tax their necessities. That is the real choice—br.ead or tobacco? Meat or motors? Cheese or whisky? Living or land?

" Ah ! my good Man," said the critic of the Budget, " but you will have to pay more for your tobacco and your whisky." " That may be," said the working man, "but in my house there's only one that drinks whisky ard smokes tobacco, while there's terr that eats bread." * * _ _ _

The Lord Chief Justice of England has provided temperance advocates with a stock argument for which they will be grateful. It was a remarkable expression of opinion from a man in such high authority when he said, at a public meeting in connection with the Alcoholic Congress on Wednesday night, "that after thirty years' experience at the Bar and ten years as a Judge he had found as a fact that 80 per cent. of crime in this country depended upon intemperance." * * *

The House of Commons last Friday after- noon passed the Trade Boards Bill through its remaining stages,after a rather weakly- supported attempt had been made.to intro- duce the fiscal issue by the back door, as Mr. Curran well put it. We sincerely hope that the effect of the Bill will be to get rid of the evil of sweating, an evil, we may note, which appears in all countries, whatever their fiscal syste-rii may chance to be. The Board of Trade can be trusted to work the Bill in the best possible manner, and we shall be very disappointed if the experiment is not a successful one.

I am convinced that it is only through peace that the highest moral and material progress of the nation can be secured.—His .Majesty the King. There are no plants or flowers "down south," and that is what we missed most.— Lieutenant Shackleton.

The British are our friends, as we are theirs, but on the field of sport everybody for himself.—President Fallieres.

We• will stand no mincing ; and unless Lord Lansdowne and his landlordly friends choose to eat their own mince up again, Parliament will be dissolved.—Mr. Winston Churchill on the Peers and the Budget. *

The Garden City at Hampstead is now generally considered to be the finest example of a city of this kind to be seen in the world. In company with Mr. Henry Vivian, M.P., Earl Grey recently paid a visit to the city, and expressed himself highly delighted with its appearance and the arrangements. A few days ago the garden city was visited by the German municipal deputation which came over to investigate municipal life and progress in this country. They were much surprised and charmed with what they saw at the Hampstead garden city, and. frankly admitted that they had nothing approaching it in Germany. Mr. Henry Vivian, M.P., and his colleagues may well be proud of their work at the Hampstead garden city. * * *

Anyone would gather from the debates in the House of Commons during the last 'fort- night that the Increment Tax was a tax that would drive the landlord into the workhouse and the tax-collector into a lunatic asylum. And yet this is the judgment of Mr. Bernard Mallett on the working of the Increment Tax -at Frankport, where it runs hiWl as 26 per cent.: "I did not discover aMy reprobation of it, as ' Socialistic ' or oppressive, .or as having checked business transactions in real estate." On the contrary, the tax seems to be especi- ally easy to collect: "It was stated that the tax was worked without friction, and it was specially commended on the ground of facility and cheapness of collection." Frankfort alone is already obtaining from this tax £50,000 a year. . .

* The other day a remark was made by the Chancellor of the Exchequer to the effect that his financial proposals had given a check to spirit-drinking which, as far as his know- ledge went, was unparalleled. Those who conduct restaurants of the highest class or who are engaged in the management of clubs are very well aware of an extra- ordinary decrease in the consumption of wine and spirits. The managing director of one of our most famous restaurants stated the ogler day, "Country Life" says, that in his prosperous concern the number of those who took luncheon or dinner had very largely increased, but that the individual bill was much smaller. The case of one of thq most fashionable clubs is even more illuminating. Twelve years ago, with 500 members, the. Wine and spirit account reached £1,300 per annum; but last year, with double the num- ber of members, the account had shrunk to less than £1,400.

Mr. Joseph Howes; who has been doing yeoman service in the High Peak division, will address an open-air meeting at Bridge Foot (near Arpley Cannons) on Monday evening, when he will reply to some of the statements made at the recent Tory meeting.

* * * Another of the Burnley brigade of tariff reform lecturers was holding forth at Bridge Foot on Thursday evening but his views did not meet with much encouragement and he was subjected to a perfecl. fusillade of questions. Workingmen in Warrington are not at all enamoured of the Protectionist nostrums and the speaker was plied with awkward queries, in which it was inevitable that "the land" should have a part. When asked for • infor- mation on the German land system and its advantages to the municipality, the speaker discreetly replied that he "was not an encylopfedia," ingeniously adding that land reform might be just as easily accom- plished under tariff reform conditions as under Free Trade. But how these tariff reformers wail about the land clauses., of the Budget! * * * The late Sir Wilfrid Lawson, whose memory is to be perpetuated by a statue on the Embankment, which the Prime Minister unveiled on Tuesday, inherited his hatred of strong drink from his father. who gave proof of his own convictions on the subject by pouring the whole contents of his cellai into a pond. At one time (says the " Westminster Gazette ") Sir Wilfrid attempted a collection of the epithets which his crusade against drink had procured him,but soon found the task too big for him, and had to give it up.— It is interesting to recall that in the early part of the reign of George I. there were two Wilifrid Lawsons sitting at West- minster, one of whom was M.P. for Cocker- mouth, a constituency represented by the late baronet more than a century and a half later. Farther back still, in the reign of Queen Elizabeth,there was a Wilfred Lawson sitting for the same county in Parliament, and a Sir Wilfrid Lawson, Kt., was also returned for Cockermouth in the Long Parliament. Fifty years ago, when Sir Wilfrid first entered the House of Commons, Disraeli welcomed him as "a chip of the old block."

OPEN-AIR MUSICAL FESTIVAL.

SUCCESSFUL EFFORT FOR THE IN

Thousands of the townspeople flocked to Bank Park I on Thursday evening when the first annual open-air musical festival promoted by the Warrington 'lnfirmary Workingmen's Committee in aid of the funds of the institution, was held, and fully realised all expecta- tions. The Committee had succeeded in enlisting the services of the Warrington Male Voice Choir, the Apollo Male Voice Choir, and. Crosfield's Brass Band, and it is impossible to speak too I highly of the public spirit of each organisation, whose members took up the project with such whole-hearted enthusiasm. Splendid arran,gements had been made and the programme was calculated to appeal. to all lovers of music. The only cause for anxiety was the fick,leness of the elements,and the lower- ing clouds which appeared just before the Commence- ment of the concert did not tend to inspire con- fidence. .However, about seven o'clock there were occasional gleams of sunshine and when Crosfleld's band took their places in the stand the park was densely thronged. Over 5,000 persons must have passed into the grounds,and the Collecting boxes, con- veniently placed at each entrance, reaped a good harvest of coins. The chairs, for which a small extra charge was made, were very well patronised. Amongst those who attended the I concert were the Mayor (Alderman Forshaw), Alder- man Burton (chairman of the Workingmen's Com- mittee), Mr. George Walker (vice-chairman), Mr. J. H. J. Hampson (hon. secretary), Mr. W. Daintith (member of the Board of Management), Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, Rev. C. Harvey Cook, Dr. E. A. Fox, Mr. J. H. Smethurst, and Mr. and Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes. The programme opened with the sprightly march "Honest Toil" (Rimier), by the band, which was immediately succeeded by that old ~... favourite the "Poet and Peasant" overture (Suppq). A remark- ably fine performance was the " Tannhauser" selection (Wagner) which was characterised by skilful execution and great expression and called forth warm appreciation.. Another popular item was the descriptive piece "A Sailor's Life' (S. Cope) which gave an effective representation of a storm and a naval battle. The concluding band selection was "William Tell" (Rossini) which included the famous storm movement. Under Mr. G. F. Bedforth's condactorship, the intricacies of this difficult piece were surmounted and the interpretation gave the utmost. pleasure and satisfaction. The two male voice choirs sang combined, under Mr. Harry 13errey's conductorship, and provided a rich muSical treat which was much appreciated. It was unfortunate that while the vocalists were on the platform in readi- ness for their second item rain fell in torrents, but they stuck to their posts heroically and the majority of the crowd also stayed. It was only a shower, and the remainder of the programme was carried through without interruption. The choirs sang the stirring "Soldiers' Chorus" from " Faust " (Gounod) accom- panied by the band, with fine effect. Their other selections were "Must I then depart from Thee" (Abt), " On the Ramparts" (Saintes), " Destruction of Gaza " (De Rille), " Beleaguered " (Sullivan), " Martyrs of the Arena " (De 1?ille), and" Soldier's Farewell" (Hatton). Each item was artistically interpreted, the voices blending beautifully, and there was a consensus of opinion that the pieces had been well chosen.

We are pleased to hear from the Secretary that the sum realized by the concert was £46 Bs. d.

BRITISH SHIPPING AND ITS GAINS UNDER FREE TRADE.

GERMAN TESTIMONY.

The German Empire has in one respect paid the British policy of Free Trade the compliment of imita- tion,which is proverbially the sincerest form of flattery. Germans have a healthy ambition to acquire a great merchant fleet. On general principle they are Pro- tectionists. They heavily protect their agriculture, at the cost of making the food of the masses of the people about one-fifth dearer than it is in this country. They protect their manufactures as heavily in proportion. But they make one notable exception. They admit free of Tariff duty all imported foreign materials, whether raw or manufactured, which are to be used in shipbuilding. Thereby they admit that, whether Free Trade be wise or unwise in regard to their inland industries, it is the only wise policy for the creation of a merchant shipping fleet which is intended te earn and pay its way in the face of the world's com- petition.

===== 0002601_19090724_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
AMERICAN EXPERIENCE.

-- A greater nation than even Germany—the United_ States, namely—has given similar testimony to us, which if unwilling is equally valuable. The States ought to have a merchant fleet as great as ours, con- sidering their material resources and the energies and abilities of their people. In the earlier part of last century the probabilities seemed to be that they would run us a neck-and-neck race towards the chief position in the ocean carrying trade. So lately as 1860 they owned 2,500,000 tons of ocean shipping to our 4,500,000 tons. Since then their tonnage has continu- ously diminished, whilst ours has grown. Now they have less than 1,000,000 tons, whilst we have 11,500,000 tons. Why is this American decrease, against so vast an increase on our side? Americans themselves admit that the reason why is their -system of Protect- tion. Steel plate and other shipbuilding essentials are so thoroughly protected, in the interests of the Steel Trust and other organisations of shortsighted selfishness, that a first-class merchant steamer costs them to build at least 40 per cent. more than it costs us in this country. Of course, they have to charge freightage rates proportionately higher. The result is that British ships carry, and earn freights upon, the. great bulk of American imports and exports, not only as between the States and this country, but as between the States and all the rest of the world. HALF THE WORLD'S SHIPPING BRITISH. According to the most recent statistics, the total steamship onnage owned by all nations is about 19,250,000 tons. Of this the -United Kingdom owned rather more t han 9,500,000 tons ; being nearly equal to that owned. by all other countrio: including thet British Colontea. we include sailing ships as well as steamers, their the total ownership in the United Kingdom is 11,250,000 tons, as compared with 14,500,000 tons for all other countries. Sailing hips, ai most people are aware, become fewer every year in proportion ta steamers. For carrying purposes, each steam ton is equal to three sail tnis—by reason, of course, of the greater speed and certainty of steam. Next to .the British ownership of 11,250,000 tons steam and sail, the largest figure for one country is the German ownership of 2,500,000 tons—less than one quarter of the British tonnage. On the other hand, the German tonnage is nearly three times as great as that of the United States. Seting that the population of the States is one third larger than that of Germany, and that the natural resources of the States exceed those of Germany in a far greater proportion, one would presume that the ocean preponderance as between the two ought to be the other way. The one probable explanation of the facts is that Germany pursues a Free Trade policy at least as regards ship- building, whilst the States are Protectionists; and also, that in other matters than shipbuilding, Germany though Protectionist, is less strenuously so than the United States. It must be noted that the actual British predomin- ance in ocean traffic is more decided than the fore- going figures indicate, because British shipping is, on the average, newer and more efficient than that owned by other countries. British ship-owners every Year sell second-band to foreign owners, numbers of vpssels which have seen a few years' service; and these second-hands' are replaced by the up-to-date ships, which, as every newspaper reader is aware, tend con- stantly to be bigger and faster than their predecessors. TWO-THIRDS OF THE WORLD'S SHIPBUILDING

BRITISH. Of course, this country also builds and sells to foreign owners new vessels as well as second-hand. As we build all the vessels we need for our own use, and sell to other countries besides, it follows that British predominance in shipbuilding is even more marked than in shipownmg. The most recent statistics shew the new ships built in all countries to .total about 3,250,000 tons per annum • of which the. British shipyards produce nearly 2,000,000 tons. That is, two-thirds of all the ships in the world are British-built. THE GAINS FROlf SHIPPING: A PIZOTEC-

TIOUIST PUZZLE EXPLAINED. how much per annum is earned for British capi- talists and workers by these 11,250.000 tons of shipping? Sir Robert Giffen estimates that British ships' earnings may fairly be reckoned at £l2 per ton per annum for steamships and .7/1 per ton for sailing ships. • Applying these figures to the tonnages already stated, and making allowances for severer competition, and lower freights since the official inquiries, it is clear that the total earnings of British shipping may be put roughly and roundly at £100,000,000 sterling per annum; being equal to £2 ss. per annum for each man, woman, and child in the United Kingdom. Now, this £100.000,000 earned by British shipping goes far to explain a puzzle which greatly troubles the minds of our Protectionists. They point to the fact that according to the Board of Trade returns year after year the value of all the goods imported into the United Kingdom (gold included) is at least - 1150,000,000 per annum more than the value of all the goods that are exported (gold included).' how is that? they ask. _

One iZrt of this apparent balance against us of -9,190,000,000 per annum is explained by the circum- stance that many hundred millions sterling of British capital are investeck in foreign countries and in British territories beyond seas ; and the interest thereupon comes to us in the shape of imported merchandise, which has not to be paid for by corresponding exports or merchandise. The other part of the supposed adverse balance is covered by our shipping earnings. _ •

Ir ft, the 1-100,000,000 per annum earned by our shipping amounts to the same thing, pecuniarily, as if we exported another £1,000,000 per annum worth of merchandise. The overwhelming bulk, both of our imports and exports, are carried in British ships. As regards the value of the merchandise imported, the figures given by the Board of Trade include not only the original cost price, but also the charges for ship freight. Since these charges are paid to British shipowners, it is obvious that the charges have not to be met by exports of other British. commodities. Again' as regards the value of the merchandise exported, the Board of Trade figures do not include the cost of the shipping freightage. Nevertheless, the freightage also has to be paid to British ship- owners. But ultimately it is charged to and paid by that foreign purchasers of our exports. They pay by sending us goods which, of course, are reckoned along with .our other imports. Thus, the whole .earnings of our shipping, about ;E.100,000,000 per annum as stated, come to us as part of the total imports of merchandise recorded in the Board of Trade returns. And thus the Protectionist vision of a huge ever-growing adverse balance of trade debt disappears like the vision it is.

A WEIGHTY WINNER AT WINWIGK.

A Lady of 25 Stones Who Won a Race.

In the course of a report which a deputation of tho Salford Board of Guardians presented yesterday, on a visit to Winwick Asylum, it was mentioned incidentally that one of the Salford patients was a woman weighing 25 stones, and this woman was the winner of a race in the sports, which were in progress while the deputation were there. - - -

• Mrs. Cruickshank, who reported this interesting fact, moved the Board to laughter by adding in an appeal to, Arr. Bratherton, the chairman of the infirmary Com- mittee, "Why can't you do the same?' Mr. Byrne: If Mrs. Cruickshank means that the Guardians should have a race, I should be very pleased to act as judge. (Laughter.) Mrs. Cruickshank: I meant the inmates. It was reported that the total number of patients at Winwick Asylum was 2,323.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Tuesday, Patrick Gill, Hoyle-street, was charged with stealing sums of money amounting to £1 Ss. 9d., the property of Thomas Warburton, Pierpoint-street.—The Chef Constable 'sta.ted that in 00tob3r' 1908, prisoner stlrted a trip club, which he styled Crystal Palace Club, with the object of visiting London in April to witness the final tieifor the English Cup. A number of workmates, including Warburton, paid to him various sums of money weekly. On the day the club was supposed to go to London the prisoner,absconded with the money, and had not ken seen until he was arrested in Bishop Auckland the previous night. A remand was necessary for a week.—Mr. C. H. Unsworth, who appeared for prisoner, applied for bail, but tho applica- tion was refused.—Prisoner was accordingly reunnied for a week. AN EARLY SEPARATION—SEQUEL TO " PLATONIC " FRIENDSEIIP.

Charles Henry Fairclough. of Liverpool, was sum- moned by his wife Annie Alice to show cause why a s.?paration should not be granted on the ground of desertion.—Mr. J. W. Knowles, of Widnes, represented the complainant and Mr. Unswortb, of Warrington, appeared for the defendant.—Mr. Knowles stated that the couple were married at St. Ann's Church,Warrington on the 13th April of this year. They went to Llan- dudno for a short honeymoon, while there they <ravelled the defendant explaining that he was "going to be boss." Complainant's father bought a shop in Hood-lane, Sankey, at a cost of £3OO and gave it to the girl so that she would have a good start.—The defendant had told the complainant's parents that he was a pawnbroker's assistant and he could earn £2 per week at the business. The first Sunday after coming back from their hog ej moon, defendant received a letter from a widow acquaintance and his wife managed too get hold of it. The letter contained an invitation for defendant to go and see her, commencing with "Dear Charlie "and saying that Dolly, a grown-up daughter would be out at three o'clock. This woman although a widow was an active woman and the letter getting into the hands of complainant put the fat into the fire, Discussion arose between the defendant and the girl's father in the course of which defendant said that his mother came first in his esteem, the widow second, and his wife third. It was rather an unpleasant beginning. However the party went to the premises in flood-lane and things went on from bad to worst. Defendant said that he was going to have the shop. He further stated that nobody in his family had ever been imprisoned but he would be, as be was going to have her life. On the 27th of April defendant left her end went to the house of the widow. He, however, came back but on Whit Monday he went away to Liverpool and saw his mother and there received another letter from hisl widow friend. His wife knew that because defendant had admitted it to her, because semetimes his tongue ' went faster than his mind. He returned home late that night and began to abuse his wife and a Mrs. Tonge who weut to the shop at the time for some ham heard this man shouting at his wife, but being rather deaf she was unable to catch what he said to her. The defendant opened the door to Mrs. rouge and she saw the complainant in a state of distress. Defendant left his wife on the 31st of Ma) and had done nothing to support her. The ingenious defence would be that the defendant was out of work. The man wanted to live a life of laziness and live on the proceeds of the little shop. In conclusion Mr. Knowles asked that a separa- tion order he granted and a substantial sum allowed to the wife per week.—Evidence was given in support of his statement.—Mr. Unswortb, in his defence, endeavoured to point out that the defendant did not intentionally desert his wife, as be had gone to look for work. Defendant said he was wilting to take his wife back if her parents would let them alone and keep ste.-ay from them.—Mr. Knowles called defendant's attention to the phrase "Platonic friend," this being the way be had referred to the widow. Mr, Knowles asked what "platonic friend" meant.—. Defendant: A friend in -every sense of the word and nothing further.—The Bench granted a separation order, defendant to pay his wife 15s. per week.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT ASSOCEATION.

INDIVIDUAL MERIT COMPETITION.

This competition, which has been a grelt success, was brought to a close on Saturday when betwe.n 400 and 500 spectators witnessed some excellent games. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Rarely his such a sensational collapse been witnessed on the Arpley enclosure as that which marked Satur- day's League match, St. Helens Rees, last ' year's champions, being dismissed in forty minutes for the insignificant total of 14 runs. The debacle was due to the brilliant bowling of the Rev. H. Gray whose keen judgment, combined with the treacherous nature of the pitch, made his deliveries practically unplay- able; the visitors hopelessly failinc, to time him. His analysis worked out at 7 wickets for nine runs, and the performance is the more remarkable when it.is stated that at the outset he had six runs hit off him without securing a wicket.

Warrington responded with 121 and Mr. Gray also had the satisfaction of heading the score list with a splendidly compiled 34. S. Partridge played with great freedom and dash for his 31 and J. W. Pearson's innings of 26 was characterised by sound judgment.

There was a good attendance, which was gratifying in view of the fact that the match was set apart for the benefit of Cresswell, the home professional. ---'

Warrington 2nd went down at St. Helens, and by virtue of their victory the home eleven are practically assured of the championship of the second division. Plinston and Bennett bowled well for Warrington and J. W. Bainbridge gave a dashing display with the bat for 47.

Other matches in the West Lancashire League resulted in victories for Earlestown and Prescot at the expense of Newton and St. Helens respectively in both the first and second divisions.

The following are the League tables up-to-date FIRST DIVISION.

St. James's look like retaining the championship of the Warrington and District League, for they have yet to see their first defeat of, the season. On Saturday, they placed an easy victory to their credit at the expense of Whitecros:s, for they passed the wiremen's total with nine wickets in hand. C. Holt took seven wickets for 17 rims, while J. H Constable batted in his usual fine style for 32. C. Lavell was top scorer for Whitecross with eleven.

Cheshire Lines Engineers, who also have a very creditable record, were victorious over the " Guardian " eleven by 13 runs. Deeley took six wickets for eleven runs and J. Duckworth knocked up 15.

Wilderspool Brewery weievell beaten by Latchford Baptists who got them all out for 29 and then com- piled 40 for five wickets. - C. Wood was the most successful batsman with 19 and W. Bennett captured six wickets for twelve runs.

In the Warrington Junior LeagUe a low-scoring match between Orford and Rylands' 2nd resulted in a victory for the villagers. Jolley took eight wickets for three runs.

There were not as many friendly matches as usual. Highfield Tannery entertained Latchford Baptists and defeated them by 23 runs. Stockton Heath Wesleyans triumphed over Bewfley-road Wesleyans, F. Bell (15) being the top scorer. Wilderspool Juniors accounted for Leigh Villas, F. Stringer (Wilderspool) and R. Shakeshaft (Leigh Villas) batting well for 22 and 26 respectively.

Lymin inflicted a severe trouncing on Sale 2nd, T. Marsh carrying out his bat with the brilliant total of 85.

There was an exciting struggle between Moore Juniors and Sankey Bridges, Moore winning by a single run. The moAt notable feature of the match was J. Bellfield's fine innings for 46.

Bowling. The remarkable popularity of this healthful spot has been More than maintained during the present season and Warrington bowlers have been kept busy both in local and county competitions. The Works Competion, which is nearing its closing stages, has been attended with complete success, and the officials and committee are to be congratulated upon the excel- lence of their arrangements.

The fourth round was brought to a closeon Saturday when Richmond's and WhitecrosS triumphed over Monks Hall No. 1 and Cheshire Lines respectively.

•Richmond's began with a 15 points advantage over the forge team and succeeded hi winning by .16 points. There was a capital game between W. Kenwright (Richmond's) and R. Jefferson (Monks, Hall). The latter started well and had the fine lead of 11-3. Then Kenwright climbed to 6 and his opponent got 2 more. From this stage Kenwright had the game to himself and is to be congratulated on winning by 15-13.

P. Antrobus (Richmond's) and S. Deakin (Monks, Hall) were also closely watched. They peeled at 6, 7,9 and 12, and then Deakin got one ahead. Antrobus, however, was on the elect and obtaining a double and a single ran out victor by 15-13.

Whitecross, last year's champions, are going strong. On Saturday they conceded Cheshire Lines 5 points and placed a handsome victory to their credit, the majority being 57. As the score indicates, the match was one-sided, Whitecross winning all the games hut two.

There was an interesting contest ,between J. Gleave

LtNDON'S NEW EXPERIENCE.

The visit of the Home and Atlantic Fleets to the Thames marked an epoch in the history of the Navy. Never before had such an armada been gathered together in London's great waterway. One hundred and fifty vessels of the Home and Atlantic Fleets have been brought together this week for the edification of the Londoner. Stretch- ing from Westminster Bridge to the Nore, and comprising every class of vessel frozn Dreadnoughts to submarines, this mighty armada as excited extraordinary enthusiasm in the Metropolis, and Southend-on-Sea, which is the centre of interest, has been almost overrun with visitors since 0 Saturday.

THE ATE DON CARLOS.

Don Carlos, Duke of Madrid, who claimed to be the lei,itimate King of Spain by the title of Charles VII., has died at Veresa of heart failure. When he was only twenty years of age Don Carlos inherited • the position of "Pretender to the Spanish throne," on which he might have sat but for his refusal to make terms when Isabella had to flee from Spain in 'lB6B.

A NEW ADULT SCHOOL.

The Adult School movement is popular because it meets the needs of all classes, is quite unsectarian in its methods of working, serves a valuable educational purpose, and encourages Bible study. The progress of the Warrington Adult School, in Academy-street since its formation well within two years ago, has been very marked and another school was inaugurated last Satur- day evening in Greening's Dining Hall, Bewsey. No better' place for such an institution could have been chosen. The meeting on Saturday evening was presided over by Mr. Harold GRAY who in the course of a speech dealing with the aims of the Adult School said it did not occur to some of them that there were enough organisations, There seemed to be a multiplication of organisations and one sometimes wandered whether we had not come to the end. However, there was ample room for an Adult School. Already we had had some little experience • in Warrington of en Adult School which had done well, and there was no reason why another should not be established in Warrington. There was no denying the fact that our Churches and Chapels had not succeeded in drawing the working classes. We sometimes believed that the working classes were irreligious, but in his opinion they were quite as religious as ever they were and quite capable of responding if suitable means and the right methods were laid before them. The Adult School was not in anyway a class movement, but it was an exceedingly suitable instrument for the working classes to-day. It was a movement that bad been tried for a number of years, especially in the Midlands They were not ecclesiastical and they had not the cold dogma upon them.f They realised that every individual had a responsibility to discharge to the community and they thought it well and fitting that they should take up subjects of social importance such for instance as the land question. They studied those subjects in a way that did not interfere with party politics. There was some work for,the Adult School to do 'in relation to the Housing problems. It was essentially a religious movement and from that one great centre came the many sided things of wlrich he had been speaking. The Adult School was tll'e place where the principles of our Lord Jesus Christ could be put into practice. (Applause). Mr. J. A. RANSOME, president of the Academy-street Adult School, said that there were 100 names on the books of his school, and they had an average attendance of over 50. He also observed that Bewsey was.the best place they could have chosen for such a school. Messrs. Bradley, J. Parry, Joseph Ellvrell, Paling, John Ellwell and A. Spittle testified to the good they had dprived from such a schbol. Each of them hoped that a school in Bewsoy would greatly prosper. During the meeting Mr. James Parry rendered "Throw out the lifeline," the audience of about 50 taking up the chorus. On a vote being put to the meeting it was decided that a school should open next day, commencing at 8.45 a.m. Sunday morning raw the foundation of the school, when about 20 members wore enlisted.

IWARRINC CHAPTERS. >

THE GREAT lIAILSTORM OF FIFTY YEARS AGO.

iffy years ago last Saturday—on July 18th, 1859 Warrington received a shock which lingered in its memory for many years, and which remains yet we believe in the memory of its oldest inhabitants. Between five and six in the evening a, thunderstorm broke over the town, and fol- lowing heavy rain a hailstorm of peculiar vehemence occurred. Erratic in its course the storm poured out its vial of wrath upon Warrington, leaving the surrounding towns and even the villages a mile away untouched. The falling of record hailstones in the midst of a period of unusually hot weather was a problem which might have puzzled wiser heads than those who endeavoured the solu- tion half a century ago. It seems worth while to mark the jubilee of this striking event by including an account of it in our Warrington Chapters. We quote first the description of an eye witness whol wrote—we think—for a Manchester news- paper. _ _

In one of his lectures Mr. Beamont tells of a Latchford woman who dated the births of her children and other events notable in her lifetime as "so long before or after the the flood "much to the astonishment of a stranger who was questioning her, until he found that the flood which she meant was not the Flood, but a flood which proved distructive to Warrington. Any local savant anxious to follow her example and discard the usual wino domini computation, may fix upon the great hailstorm of Monday last, as the starting point of a chronology more likely to be remembered by Warrington worthies than the era of Nabonasser, the Olympiads of the Greeks, the building of Rome, or the Hegira of the Mahommetans. "Monday last, like a week or more of its predecessors, was stifling in the extreme. Everybody had gone to the seaside who could go, and those who could not, bore their mis- fortune with an ill grace. Ices were down, to a halfpenny, and " pop " up as high as ginger and coloric could send it,when towards five in the afternoon the premonitory symptoms of a thunderstorm appeared in the shape of a lowering sky, and half suppressed grumblings. Soon after five o'clock the thunderstorm was very audible, as it rapidly followed the vivid lightning, thus betokening its close proximity to War- rington. Rain then descended, filling the hearts of all with gladness ; but soon it gave place to hail of the ordinary kind; and it, in its turn, to large lumps of ice, which rattled on the ground as one could fancy a shower of marbles from a two-mile altitude would do.

"About a quarter to six the wind rose, and then such a scene of destruction to glass com- menced as was never witnessed in Warring- ton before. Every skylight and almost every window with a northern or western aspect began to be riddled as if a regiment of volun- teers were at rifle practice. For a full quarter of an hour this firing continued; the hail, or rather the pieces of ice being, in thousands of instances, larger than marbles, and many of them so large as to lay our words open to doubt if we named the size. From the cessation of the storm the streets became thronged by anxious onlookers, and every glazier's shop was besieged by applicants. From the Parish Church to Bank Quay Station, a distance of nearly a mile and a half, the windows on the left hand side were more or less riddled and all back rows fared equally as bad. Trinity Church presented a deplorable sight, some 200 squares being knocked out. Bold-street Chapel fared rather better. Cairo-street, Golborne-street and St. John's Chapels escaped almost unimpaired; but not so St. James' Church, Latchford. Upon the side fronting Warrington the storm seemed to have expended its strength, and probably 2,000 of the little squares were shivered in pieces. "The public works suffered considerably. At Messrs. Stubs about 1,000 squares of glass were broken. At Messrs. Bashall and Sons 3,000 square feet of new glass were required and great damage was done by the broken glass cutting the yarn, the hands losing a day's Work in consequence. "Of the tales of loss by flood in private life time would fail us to tell. Cisterns were overflowed here, roof-gutters there, the kitchen flooded in a third. The annoyance through rain and hail finding an entrance through skylights was very general and in numerous cases the floors of upper rooms were flooded and the ceilings soaked. The most remarkable case which has come to our knowledge is that of Mr. Mallett, draper; the roof was struck by lighting and through the aperture there poured hail which covered and greatly damaged his stock. 'Among greenhouses the destruction was widespread. Mr. E. Bolton's loss at his garden at Bank Quay exceeds 6,000 squares and Coloner Patten's and Alderman Peter Rylands' many thousands. Numerous gardens have been all but destroyed for the season. The little paradise attached to Alderman Thewlis' residence was a picture of desolation, so also was Mr. Marsh's." The storm appears to have singled out Warrington and Warrington only as the scene of attack. A mile out of the town on every side the storm passed over harmlessly except that windows were broken at Paddington and Woolston. At Wilderspool Brewery only one pane of glass was broken and no other damage occurred. In Warring- ton it was computed that 40,000 panes had been broken. Glaziers were summoned from the country around and quite a small army were imported from Manchester, but it was a full fortnight before the town lost its besieged appearance. The scribe from whom we have been quoting continues: "After the storm,good humour was pretty generally exhibited by all, especially those who were not sufferers. Jokes were as freely cracked las the glass had been, chiefly on the paneful nature of the affair, and the paneless process taken, no one fortunately being hurt. A good deal of inquiry was made as to where the hailstones came from, the weather being intensely hot; and the most satisfactory answer which we heard was that which likened the Process of ice making in the clouds, to that daily adopted in London, where ice is made by steam, and the better the furnace the more the ice which is pro- duced, the caloric, by the aid of ether being *extracted from the cold water. "On one ' point the inhabitants agree, including the oldest one, who was especially enquired after, and that is that there never was the like of it before, and the pious wish is added that there may never be the like of it again." Animated discussions arose as to responsi- bility for the damage. It is usual for tenants to put in broken glass and the owners refused to take responsibility. The tenants argued that the damage was beyond their control and should be treated as damage done to roofs and chimneys. - -

How these keen diseuisj..ons ended we do

not know, but the absence of any record of litigation suggests that they were amicably settled. The town got over its loss as good humouredly as could be expected but not for many years did it forget the sharp vengeance of the brief and angry Midsummer storm of 1859.

The theme was an attractive one for poets —of whom the town has never been entirely deficient—and we have been fortunate enough to unearth a rhyme which the occasion inspired.

WARRINGTON " HAIL." " All hail ! Macbeth."

Conic hither Pegasus:let's mount thy bare back, There's a great event happened needs record in rhyme, Tho' I fear I shall find thee halt as an old hack,

Through running the late £lOO match ag'nst time. No matter—if Tennyson's trotting bath galled thee I'll e'en be content to hang by thy tail; And if his old lady's strange chatter appalled thee, I'll Cheer up thy spirits with Warrington's hail. MISCELLANY.

The "Daily Post and Mercury" had an interesting paragraph last week concerning the Oberlin Press of WarritTton. We quote the paragraph, promising some day to tell our readers all about Philip Carpenter and his numberless hobbies. Mr. W. W. Yates, of Dewsbury, .(himself an old Warrington journalist) writes "My feelings have been deeply stirred—old memories being revived—through reading in your 'News, Notes and Queries' column what two correspondents have penned respecting a song containing the words, 'When the might with the right and the truth shall be.' One gives the last verse, but not quite correctly; and, with your per- mission, I quote it as printed in the first booklet in which it appeared, entitled 'Songs of Progress and Affection for the People' (Oberlin Press, Warrington, 1847) : Let good men ne'er of truth despair, Though humble efforts fail, _ But give not o'er until once more - . .

The righteous cause prevail, - In vain and long-enduring wrong The weak may strive against the strong, But the day will yet appear When the might with the right and the truth shall be, &c.

The Oberlin Press was a department—shall I call it ?--of an Industrial School established by the Rev. Philip P. Carpenter, pastor of the Unitarian Chapel in Cairo-street, War- rington, at a time of acute distress. In a little periodical styled The Helper' (1850), Philip, as he was affectionately called by all classes, recounted the origin of his print- ing project. "Our press," he said, " was a rickety old wooden instrument; our type was worn; and none of understood our business." But he and the out-of-works he was assisting persevered, and a long period of depression in trade was not only tided over, but several of the young men lifted, through what they learned in the Industrial School, into positions in life they could not otherwise have attained. Philip was the first to take the composing stick in hand, and it is worth recording that the lines he set were these, so descriptive of the good man:—

I slept and dreamed that life was Beauty; I woke and found that life was Duty. ** * *

Chemists get some curious commissions rom juvenile messengers as everyone knows, and one recently given in Stockton Heath deserves to be recorded. A maiden appeared at the counter and made request for( dislo- cated cocoanut." The chemist found it an impossible order and the maiden tried again. If it was not the dislocated article it was "desecrated cocoanut." This was quite clear enough for the shopman who handed over some dessicated cocoanut—the correct article.
