A CITY GRAVEYARD.

By Rosa E. Gladding.

' You cannot see it from the busy High Street though it is not a hundred yards away, and its existence is unsuspected from the front of the houses that back on to it three sides of the square. Just inside the gates the path starts with every intention of keeping straight but like many another opening with as fair a promise, after the first swerve aside runs down the hill reck- lessly until it is stopped by the ,low wall which prevents it tumbling over to the cottages at the foot. One would think these houses and those on the sloping sides of the square would not be particularly eligible or healthy, yet they have no more sickness here than in any other part of the town, and seldom is one of them to let.. Perhaps the bit of dings/ green that fills up the spaces between the gravestones has some- thing to do with the popularity of the place, grass grows so sparsely on these bleak grey hills. More- over there is a solitary sycamore tree somewhere about the middle of the place which lifts its feeble arms at the stirring of the spring and after stretching itself tentatively, surprised to find itself still wakened by the call, puts forth a few bronze, fan-shaped leaves. A dreary spot is the burying-ground at the back end of the year. The grass, lush and dank, falls with its own weight under the fitful sweep of the wind into strange hollows and caves, like graves dug by elfish hands for folk unwaringly stepping that way; and the dripping rains that wash, the grimy roofs and pave- ments clean seem never to move the dark stains from these stones, _ _

Ono touch of Nature making the graveyard kin with the living world around is a heavy fall of snow. Then the uncouth shafts and obelisks that mark the resting place of forgotten bones are clothed upon and rounded into shapes more natural and familiar ; the few headstones that have succeeded in keeping erect spite of the 'savage winds that have laid many of their fellows low are swathed with folds of beauty no human hand could drape with such grace. The broken unkept path, the Via Dolorosa of many an aching heart, is laid fair and smooth again as though never a foot had trodden its weary length. Even the heavy iron gate which swings to with such an ugly clang in the dull moaning of a coming storm is trellissed and laced ,with a fairy fretwork seen 'elsewhere but in dreams. .

Spring is heralded in the burying-ground by an unworded disturbance on its utmost fringes. Over the walls hang strips of colour of every line and size, car-pets, mats, curtains, blinds, most of them fresh from their annual bath of dye, making such brilliant patches up among the 'sober greys and greens that from the foot of the bill the dingy old square is like some hanging garden in far-off Babylon. All the windows of the surrounding houses are wide open,and neighbourly greetings pass briskly among the women who pop their heads out and shake brooms and dusters at such regular intervals one would think the whole performance from end to end of the row was done by clockwork.

Summer follows swift on the heels of the' cleaning down ' ; indeed the windows seem never wholly closed afterwards, and when the yellow stars of the dandelions appear among the grassy mounds in the square, children will come in and gather bunches 'to take to teacher.' From outothe mason's shed hard by an old bench is brought and placed on the base of the biggest monument, this being the recognised and approved rendezvous of some four or five old men from the adjacent streets who there smoke their pipes in peace and settle the affairs of nations undisturbed. A funeral in the burying-ground is the occasion for much making up of arrears among the inhabitants of the square in the matter of friendly calls. Once when an Alderman of the adjoining city was buried a real Lord Mayor wearing his chain of office was ambng the mourners, and real policeman carried the coffin. So unique was the occasion that the merest buds of intimacy blossomed out suddenly into full-blown friendships, so that the heads at the windows rose tier above tier like a miniature arena; dozens of youthful legs dangled fi ern the walls all round ; whilst the wreaths left on the grave were a Mecca of pilgrimage for the inhabitants of the district for a full fortnight after. The great gala day in the burying-ground however, is the annual mowing when the place takes on a rural and unsophisticated air in which the whole neighbour- hood shares. This mowing is a movable feast taking about four and a half days to accomplish. It is per- formed by a short, lame man with a squint, whose shirt sleeves of poppy-red keep up the agricultural illusion in which for the time we live ; his occupation for the rest of the year is altogether undiscoverable. The night before the mowing he will make his appearance carrying an old-fashioned lumbersome scythe. This he places against the wall whilst he lights his pipe and proceeds to take a leisurely and quite unnecessary survey of , the ground. Carrying the Scythe ostentatiously away when this is done he thereby whets the curiosity of the troops of youngsters at play in the narrow streets near by, and gives a new topic of conversation to the' knots of women in the doorways. All of which is in the approved manner of a travelling show parading a few of its properties on the eve of the performance proper. When fairly started on his work he is sometimes lost to sight among the tombs, the scythe being discarded in crowded places for a pair of clumsy shears. Then you will catch a glimpse of the poppy-red from which a thin blue.grey film rises where he sits smoking his pipe on one of the stones or quietly munching the dinner which his wife brings him tied up in a big blue-checked handkerchief. At the end of the day the woman comes again with a clean apron on and a child in her arms. Sometimes she will put the child down to crawl on the 0.1-ass whilst she, in a nervous shame-faced way, is ill toss the hay about a bit as though impelled, poor soul, by some remembrance of earlier happier days. Quite a heavy load is the result of the lame man's labours, the children gather- ing in a crowd to see the waggon wriggle up the rugged path and just clear the gates, leaving wisps of hay trailing from the spikes at the top. Somehow I do not like the square so well after the mowing. A sense of loss and loneliness pervades the place ; the headstones stand out gaunt and bony, and there are ugly holes ..where the children grubbed up the roots'M making dandelion tea—or stout, as the boldest of them at once and unblushingly admits. Among the few regular habitues of the burying- ground is a tall, spare gentlemanly-looking man, with a white military moustache; who comes in at all times and in all weathers. Generally he wears a thin dark overcoat buttoned up to the chin, and his boots are always carefully polished. You may come upon him leaning chin in hand against a black granite casket- shaped tomb, one of the best in the square, studying the letters cut deep on the top as though it were a riddle he fain would read. No turn of the head or movement of any kind betokens that he hears your approach, and for a full quarter of an hour or more he will keep the same immovable attitude. Then as though struck with a sudden inspiration he will proceed in sharp abrupt strides down the rough path, and stepping carefully across the mounds on one side halt-at a place where a year or so ago his mother was laid to sleep. Taking a jar from his pocket he fills it at a tap in the wall fixed for such purposes and then waters the grave carefully and thoroughly all over, sometimes when the rain is actually falling in torrents. Once on a bitter day in March when the wind blew the sleet like bits of broken glasi against his face, I saw him enter the burying-ground and without his usual perplexed pause by the tomb at the top, go down to his favourite place. Whilst performing his usual task of watering the grave the wind in a sudden burst of fury threw him face downward across the place where some day, perchance he too—the secret which has baffled his poor wit at last known—will be put to rest. Nobody laughs at the old man but a few callous school-boys who call out 'Hullo Potty' as he stands suddenly still on the busy causeway, that pathetic questioning look in his eyes; and then a wisp of a, smile will cross his face and as though the answer were found he *ill dart off to his haunt among the tombs. Seldom does he answer my greeting, but there is always a courteous bow and a lift of his hat as I pass him going ,through the gates. Some say lie was crossed in love, some that he lost a fortune, others that he was harshly treated as a boy. But the years come and go, and the stalwart sunny-haired youth who went white before his prime will find no answer to his problem nor any rest to his feet until they carry him, past the black stone slab, down the crooked path, and turn aside to the place that his love has tended so long.

MARRIED WOMEN TEACHERS.

ANOTHER NOTICE WITHDRAWN.

The question of the dismissal of married women teachers was again mentioned at the meeting of the Warrington Education Committee on Monday evening. At the General Purposes Committee meeting, held in private just prior to the meeting of the Education Committee, a resolution that the notice to terminate the engagement of Mrs. Proud la withdrawn was passed by 11 votes to seven. A resolution that the notice to terminate the engagement of Mrs. Cann be withdrawn was defeated by seven votes to eleven. Mr. UPSON moved that the minute with regard to Mrs. Cann be not approved and adopted. The DIRECTOR: You can't. Mr. UPSON: Why. The DrnEc : Because it is not a minute. It i 3 simply a record of what took place. Mr. UPSON: What right have Ito raise the question spin

-The DIRECTOR: You cannot raise it again except by special motion. Mr. UPSON : Can I. move that the record be expunged? _ _

ibe-DlREcrort : Of Course you can da that, but it will have no practical effect. Mr. UPSON : Then I will move that the minute or , he record, or whatever it be, referring to Mrs. aann Dte expunged. Ido so for several reasons and I think You will agree they are additional to those already given. The CHAIRMAN: Are you quits safe in raising this question in public meeting? The DIRECTOR said he would like to point out that the amendment proposed by Mr. Upson would not have any effect on tbe notice already given. Mr. Upson's object was to give the same treatment to ene lady as had been given to another lady. To move that the record be expunged would not gain this object. Mr. BRADSHAW said the question had been thoroughly discussed in committee, and the only object he could see in Mr. Upson's amendment was that be wanted it advertising in the papers that he was representing the teachers.

Mr. UPSON : I appeal for the protection of the cbair. I do not think I have ever done a thing of that sort. I want to submit fresh reasons why this resolution should not be carried into effect.

The DIRECTOR: The position is this: You con- sidered a certain line of action and decided not to adopt that lino. WHAT THE SALFORD COMMITTEE HAS DONE At a meeting of the Salford Education Committee on Monday night, Mr. MATHER asked whether the Committee intended to make any restriction in the ease of notice given to married teachers, seeing what had taken place at Warrington on the subject. Canon Scorr said the matter had not been before the Committee, but he should imagine not. Whathappened at Warrington was that an action was brought against the authorities there, and rather than go forward in that action they bad withdrawn their notices. The notices had expired in most of the Salford cases, and no one could bring an action. They could not bring an action to be reinstated.

The CHAIRMAN: No trouble is contemplated? Canon Scorr : No, there is no trouble contemplated. Mr. MATHER considered that the notices, for the time being, should be withdrawn. There was no doubt, be said, that Salford would have to "face the musio as Warrington had done. Canon Scorr said he did not see what could be done, The notices had expired in most cases and the teachers bad left. It would be time enough to take action when they were brought face to face with the difficulty. If there was any difficulty they must deal with each case on its merits. He did not know the details of the War- rington case, but the Committee had tried not to give notices where hardships would be caused. He did not think any persons had had to leave where it meant givinrup their main subsistence. The Chairman : lam very glad that has been said. LONDON COUNTY COUNCIL'S THREAT.

The threatened dismissal of married women teachers by the Education Committee of the London County Council was 'discussed at the annual conference of the Fabian Society, and the following resolution was passed- by a large majority :—" This Conference pro- tests against the proposed dismissal by the Education Committee of the London County Council of married women teachers, transferred for the convenience of the unattached list."

WIRELESS TELEGRAPHY IN CANADA.

Experiments in wireless telegraphy are to be under- taken on the Peel river on Yukon territory, two thousand miles north of Edmonton, and the Govern- ment experimentalist is on his way to that point to make the necessary arrangements. Direct communica- tion would be established from the station on the Peel river with Seattle via the wireless stations at Eagle, Valdez and Vancouver in British Columbia, and mitt; the other great centres on the continent.

WHOSE HAND?

By ANNIE 0. TIBBIT3.

ituthor of "The Love that Won," " Hantcd Down," "Hidden Hands," "The Finger of Fate," "Robes of Shame," &e.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: 'As a vessel is going down Olga Murray tells Dr. Dacre that, six years since, she murderer'. Stephen Henderson, of Greatbridge Hall, for which crime suspicion- fell on Richard Sterry. If possible, the statement which she signs is to be kept from her lover, Sir Geoffrey Verriker A bottle containing the document is flung into the sea. Sir Geoffrey. who rescues Olga from th( wreck, insists on taking her to his mother at fiemscote Hall, of which he has just come into possession by the death of his uncle. Mrs. Dunston was to have married the uncle. and she plots with her daughter Julia to make the latter Lady Vtrriker by a marriage with Geoffrey or Geoffrey's cousin Edward.

Dick Sterry owns the neighbouring estate to the Verrikers, and is in love with C:eoffrey', sister. As Geoffrey and Olga are nearing born: Dick gets into the railway carrige.

CHAPTER VI.

There was a banging of doors and a clamour of voices as the train began to move slowly out of the station again. The lights flashed past. The name Greatbridge,tin huge white letters on a board; swept quickly by, and then the darkness of the open country lay about them. Far off across the Lroadt,fields the sun lay dusky red near the horizon. Sir Geoffrey, bending over Olga, looked out of the window. "How strange it seems," he said, su‘ldenly, "to be coming home like this. Home!" He stared out at the strange country. Olga did not move or open her eyes, but through her closed lids she could feel the eyes of the man opposite her, could see every detail of his face. He had not stirred in his seat since he got in. Olga, with every nerve on edge, felt that she would know even if he lifted •a finger. "We shall soon be there now," Sir Geoffrey was saying, staring out of the window. "There Is about a three-mile drive from the station, and then-" He looked down at Olga suddenly. She had not opened her eyes. "Have you got a headache, Olga?" he added. PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

[By _Redcap.]

MOULTING

In a few weeks time the majority of fowls will have dropped into moult, and it is essential that they should be given proper treatment while passing through such an ordeal. There is no time in the life of a fowl Which demands so much from the system as that of growing new feathers. In fact I have known birds that have had a bard show career to die as a result of moulting, even whep under special treatment. It is not so much the casting off as the growing of the new feathers which tells on the constitution. I will ask my readers to carefully observe such growth, during the moulting period, for themselves. You will notice how when the quills start to grow and then gradually burst, sometimes with the greater pOrtion of the sheath dried into the webbing of the feather. When this happens it it a proof that the fowl is unable to supply sufficient oil to fully mature each feather. If you extract one of these quills you will find at the root an oil cell. It is from this cell that the feather is supplied with all the nutriment which goes to creaft - perfect growth. You should always feed your stock with an abundayce of oily food. If upon examination you find any of yourfoxils feather-bound,carefullyrelieve by splitting open the sheath which encloses the feather.

FEEDING.

The ordinary diet has to give place to many alter- ations.during the moulting season, because while you have been feeding for flesh-forming, you will now have to feed for internal fat, as this is the generating station of the oil which goes to supply the new feather. First of all let me remind you to add a little Epsom salts to the drinking water at least twice a week for a few weeks before the birds drop thoroughly into moult. When in the moult proper, substitute an iron tonic in the water for the salts. There is good reason for this, because if you give salts when in the moult your birds would be apt to take cold sooner as a result. If any of my readers keep white coloured birds, and intend to exhibit them after the moult, then I should recommend you not give tie iron tonic ; as it has an influence upon the colour of the feather, and most likely they would moult z out a sappy colour. The alternative would be found in giving plenty of green food and flesh meat. Now as to grains and meal. Where biscuit meal has-been used this may be con- tinued, adding to it maize-meal instead of middlings, say about three mornings per week. If biscuit meal is unobtainable for any reason then middlings will be found to be a good substitute. The best grains during moult are Indian-corn, buck- wheat and oats, with a little hempseed not mbre than twice a week. The latter contains nearly three times more oil than Indian-corn so that you will see the advisability of Using it very sparingly. Flesh-food and green food should be given in abundance to ensure a quick moult. But always see that the green food is perfectly fresh and green. Rigidly throw out decayed leaves and also old leaves, or such that have become tough and stringy. Perhaps if I detailed foods and feeding and gave it. to -you in proper order it would best serve the purpose. I will therefore risk the jokes of my contemporaries and call it the

MOULTING MENU. For the morning meal of soft food I should recommend, biscuit meal two-parts, maize-meal one part,' oatmeal, one part, scald well together with boiling water and give this diet say three mornings per week. On the other four mornings you could give the following as a change :—Aluddlings two parts, maize-meal one part, and meat-meal (about one ounce per bird). For the mid-day meal give green-food only with any scraps from the table.' The evening meal should consist of grain and given as follows.' Oats and wheat three times a week ; indian corn twice a week. The remaining two nights you could give a mixture of oats, wheat, indian corn and buckwheat and a little hempseed and give in quantity according to value mentioned. A light sprinkling of sulphur added to the soft food will also be found very beneficial in aiding your birds through the moult.

FOWLS IN CONFINED RUNS are most likely to suffer most during the moult, because those on a free range have every opportunity to obtain natural food stuffs and are thus assisted very materially. Therefore it is essential that fowls in confined runs and back yards should have special attkntion. Careful feeding and perfect cleanliness should prevail. When fowls cease to lay eggs it is often because they are neglected, and most especially during moult. You cannot expect a hen to grow feathers and lay eggs at the same time. I admit that it is so in exceptional cases but only -where you find good treatment and from stock of a good sound consti- tution. Some people seem to forget that fowls have been domesticated and that under natural conditions we should have no eggs at all in winter; not to speak of expecting eggs during moult. There is still another point that I should like to draw the attention of my readers to—that of killing fowls for table purposes whilst in moult. There is absolutely nothing gained by so doing, for you not only have More diffi- culty in dressing such birds, but at the same time they are greatly reduced in weight and quality of Ile,h. Fowls intended for culinary purposes should either be killed before a moult or not less than two or three wEeks afterwards. I have now said sufficient on moulting to givemy readers a plain idea of . treat- ment during such a process. I ask you to adopt these hints as yourlowls come into moult and you will be surprised how soon they will get over it and again commence to lay. I have a little space at my disposal so I intend to say something on

THE PRESERVATION OF EGGS,

I would not recommend preserved eggs for inarket purposes, unless of course there was a demand for them. however, they are useful for home consump- tion when eggs are scarce. There are two methods which I recommend, namely preserving in water-glass and also in lime, according to the following direc- tions :=-One pound of water-glass to .six quarts of hot water, after stirring quickly leave to cool off. 'Then pour the liquid into a large earthenware vessel and place in a cool spot. Put the eggs into the liquid daily, and keep on adding to until about an inch from the top. See to it. that all the eggs are quite fresh when put in and that none are cracked. The system of lime preservation is as follows :—Pour one gallon of water on to lib. of quick-lime. Let it stand for a day, then add 6ozs. of salt and loz. of cream of tartar, and well mix the whole together. Let the sediment settle and pour off the clear liquid into a vessel and use the same for preserving the eggs. A useful hint when using preserved eggs is to pierce the larger end with a pin. The two methods of preserving could be adopted and the one you were most successful with could be adhered to the following season. Next week I shall write on the subject of " Contr4ste between old and new breeda o# poultry."

AN EMACIATED HOESE.—Jobn Lee, of Dog Farm, Oughtrington, was summoned for allowing a horse to be worked whilst in an unfit state, and Peter Wadsworth for working the same.—lnspector Brian of the N.S.P.C.A. stated that. from information received he isited, the defendant's farm and saw the mare, attached to a cart. It's condi tic n was worse than he anticipated, for the animal was sweating and trembling, and swaying from side to side through sheer weakness. Witness asked defendant how long the horse had been in that condition 'crud he replied "for about six months." Witness bad the horse examined by a vetenary surgeon, who advised that the horse should be shot. The plsture on which the horse was grazing was fairly good but the horse was unable to digest it through•old a4e. —Mr. Harrison, a veterinary surgeon, stated that he horse was suffering from some wasting disease and could not be cured even if fed on the best of food. It would be about 20 years of age. He thought pain was inflicted on the animal when it was worked.—Defend- ant stated that he only used the mare for carting manure and'clover in and out of the fields and never used iton the roads.—The case was dismissed.

Qughtrington Flower Show and Sports. Full Prize List.

The pleasant grounds of Oughtrington PArk were on Saturday the scene of the twelfth annual flower show and sports promoted by the Ought' ington Floral and Horticultural Society. Fortunately the event was favoured with delightful summer weather and proved i a complete success n every respect. The mmy pretty gardens in the neighbourhood testify to the good influence sihich the society exerts, and on Saturday, notwithstanding the backward season, the exhibition of fruit, flowers and vegetables was most creditable. Mr. G. P. Dewhurst, the President, his always taken an active interest in the society's welfare and thanks are due to him for placing his beautiful grounds at the memt.ers' disposal for the show. THE STOCKTON HEAI H GARDEN SOCIETY.—The members of the Stockten Heath Garden Society were on Thursday evening given the opportunity of visiting a number of gardens at the back of houseseof members in Ellestnere-read. Advantage was takcn of it by a large patty, and it was generally agreed that the visit was one of the most interesting and instructive of the series. All the gardens were in a high state of cultiva- tion and evidenced the fact that their owners are enthusiastic in their attention to them. There was remarkable variety in the character of the gardens. Some were mainly devoted to the raising of useful vegetables, but in all cases there were patches for flowers which gave the garden in general qu:to an attractive and bright appearance. There aas a splendid show of roses,which included many of the new varieties,in one garden. In some of the backyards astonishing effects bad been obtained. The space available had been laid out to .the best advantage and some really creditable displays of flowers and greenery secured. The Society offer 'special prizes for these Lackyard gardens and there ' are nnmerous entries. Ample evidence was sfforded of the fact. that a beautiful garded may be arranged in a backyard and excellent floral results sot. '1 he inspection proved highly instrnctive to many of the visitors, ielding many hints and suggestions for the future culture and ievelopment of their own garden plots. This is (-me of the objects of these evening visits to other people's gardens and is a feature in connection with the Garden Society's work which is much appreciated. The arrangements for the annual show at Hill Cliffe Park on August 21st.. are already well in hand. mad it is anticipated that it will Le very successful. The competition in the varicus classes is sure to to keen. Lady Greenall will open the show.

Padgate.

WESLEY GUILD.—A very novel and interesting devotional meeting of the' Wesley Guild was held on Monday last in the open air. Mr. William Bennett, of Lyndhurst, Padgate, kindly lent his lawn for the occa- sion, and the beautiful evening and charming surround- ings made the meeting a memorable one in the Guild's history.—The Rev. T. Wadding, who occupied the chair, said how thankful the members ought to feel towards Mr. Bennett and family for their kindness in opening their grounds, and making the delightful meeting possible.—The Rev. W. C. T. Neville then gave a very interestingleeture on "English Elegy," in the course of which he reviewed some of the greatest elegies in English literature. The Lecturer asked : What do we learn from these gre•it elegies ? There can be no explanation in material terms of spiritual life. Tennyson did not believe in a religion of mystery. He talks to us about truths that cannot be proved, and tells us that the Spiritual Rock shall flow through all our deeds and make them pure. By reading these elegies we gain an elevated conception of human nature. Elegy must engage the sympathy of every plain man who lolies his friend. We get through these elegies a glimpse of the eternal. We must be in sympathy with all those who are trying to make our lives beautiful, but in so doing let us not fbrg6t the paradise that is beyond.

===== 0002601_19090724_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOULD SHEEP R.E COLOURED?

This W-a-s- one of the most discussed items in the ovine section of the Royal showyard, and it was conspicuously noticeable that foreign buyers were reluctant to have anything whatever to do with sheep that had been arti- ficially coloured. One buyer, who had made over seventy purchases, absolutely refused to purchase one of the pens of sheep in the showyard, and had to make an appointment to visit the flock at home, so that be could see them in their natural wool. The South Hams were specially singled, out for all the special adjectives in the vocabulary of sheep-buyers in particular. If a spectator happened to put his hand on the backs of a pen of Lincolcs, he had sufficient paint to set any rail- way signal at danger, and this fact warned off those who would otherwise have teen interested in the breed. A group of e'glit or nine Americans steed near one pen, making fun of the coloured sheep; they asserted that the colouring must be done to hide some defect, so rather than purchase sheenlhat they c, mid not handle they left them alone. AT all Continental shows sheep are shogn in their natural fleece. In France all show sheep that are shorn must have a patch of the previous wool left on the shoulder.—" Country Life."

CAERWYS CAMP.

Interesting Manceuvres for the Local Territorials.

The Warrington Territorials will encamp at Caerwys, Flintshire, with the West Lancashire Territorial Units, for the fortnight commencing Sunday, August Ist. It is expected that a total of 12,C00 troops of all arms will take part in the operations, which have been so devised as to enable every unit in the division to secure the greatest possible good out of the training. In some quarters exists tbe idea that a Territorial camp is a picnic on a more or less colossal scale; but a perusal of the programme still at at once dispel any such notion. There is not an idle day. Even on the Satur- day of the first week—the only half-holiday—the then will have put in quite a fair amount of work hefore being dismissed. Every unit in the division will twice be exercised in a scheme in which Artillery, Engineers, Infantry, Army service Corps troops, and Royal Army Medical Corps nen will all be engaged; base opera- tions will be followed by a scheme in which the scouts, signallers, and cyclists will be specially exercised ; ad the training will culminate in combined mancenvres extending over forty-eight hours,and in which the whole of the trooks in camp will take part. These combined operations will he carried out under the direct supervision of Major General Bethune him- self. The manoeuvring area consists of the Clwydian range of mountains on the west, and the hills lying between Caer wys and the river Dee on the east. This area comprises many square miles of rough mountain land, and is calculated to test the limbs and lungs of the troops to the utmost. In addition, an Artillery "practice camp "will be formed on the banks of the Dee near Holywell.

Local Winner at Bisley.

At the great annuual shooting meeting at B isley this week Sergeant-Drummer J.. W. Plinston, of the Warrington Territorials, was amongst the winners in the Brookwood competition, scoring, out of a possible 35, with seven shots at 200 yards. 33 and at 500 yards 34. He also obtained a prize of £2 in the first stage of the King's competition with 04 shots and an award of £1 in the Tyros (S.R.), series two, with 94 shots.

Woolston.

ATTEMPTED SUICIDE.-At the Warrington Petty Sessions, cn Wednesday, William Thornton, 79, Percival-lane, Runcorn, was chargcd with having attempted to commit suicide cn July 15th, at Woolston. The evidence showed that on the morning in question defendant wr.s found by John Stubbs, a farmer, in a field. He was bleeding from a wound on the left wrist, and had a pocket knife in his hand. Defendant was naked with the exception of his trousers. The farmer took the knife from him and the police were informed. Police Constable Metcalfe bandaged the wound, and ccnveyed the defendant to the Infirmary at Warrington. Defendant admitted having tried to commit suicide, and said he was tired of living.—Defendant was emanded to the wolkhouse for 14 days. •

Daresbary.

THE Couscir. Scrioor..—At the meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee at Crewe, on Monday, alternative plans and estimates, as prepared by the County Ar. bitect, for the alteration and enlargement of the Daresbury Council School, in accordance with the resolution of the sub-committee, and showing an estimate of £550 if infants be excluded and £6OO if they be retained at the school, were submitted. The Clerk reported that the plans and estimates had been laid before the managers of the school for their observa- tions, and he submitted a resolution passed by them, of which the following is a copy :—" That the scheme retaining the infants be recommended, hut the managers are of opinion that having regard to the continuobs decline of the numbers attending this school, and the prospects of a further reduction owing to Standard HI. being kept at Moore, the enlargement of the present bnilding is not necessary." The Rev. T. T. Lee-Jones attended before the sub-committee on behalf of the parents and residents of those parishes from which infants and Standard 1. children now attend the Daresbury School, and he submitted that there was a unanimous and strong feeling in the parishes concerned against the proposal to exclude,such children from the school, The Sub-committee had resolved—(a) That the tesolution of the 25th March, 3909, that infants and Standard I. be excluded from the Daresbury School, be not acted upon, (b) That the County Architect's plan, numbered two, for the alteration and enlargement of the school premises with the necessary provision for infants be approved and recommended to the County Education Committee for adoptitm.—The Education Committee declined to pass the foregoing resolution.

MR. EASTHAM GIVING OUT THE HYMN.

The Lymm Free Church Schools held their annual Walking Day on Wednesday.- Our photograph shows Mr. 'Eastham giving out the hymn prior to the scholars dispersing to the,various schools for tea. The route was from the Cross via Rush Green, Church Green, Brookfield, Elm Tree-road, and Booth's Hill. The schools taking part were the Congregational, Wesleyan, Independent Methodist (Zion Chapel), Primitive Methodist,and Baptist (Higher-lane). There were probably about 500 children in the procession, which was very well organised. The Lymm Subscription Prize Band (conductor, Mr. J. Nagle) led the way, and the singing was conducted by Mr. Eastham. In the evening they met in the field kindly lent by Mr. J. E. M. Grundy, where games c`i7..c. were indulged in. The sports were very good and a credit to the management of Mr. B. Edwards. Miss Welford presented the prizes.

Penketh.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.—On -111.0neay a Council meeting was held in the new Council schools, Stuck. lane commencing at 8 o'clock. There wete present, D r. Murray, J.P., in the chair Messrs. J. G. Withinshaw, J. Woodward, D. Houghton, J. Ditchfield, R. Routledge, Miss Tinsley and the Clerk, Mr. E. Foster. It was resolved that two school managers be appointed.—Mr. Withinshaw seconded, Mr. D. Houghton's proposal that Mr. R. Aldcroft be elected in place of Mr. A. Seed who had left the village ; also that that Mr. Midwood be rc-elected.—The motion was then carried. TrrE NEW. SEWER.—The chairman said he had had an interview with Mr. Newcombe who had decided to ,put the sewer deep enough to take away the sewerage from Stock-lane bridge, and the rough estimate would be about £ll9. That would he for 12in. pipes from the new schools to Faulkners' bridge. It was resolved that the payment extend ever two half-years which will mean 3ici in the 2 far each half-year. The minutes of the parish meeting were then passed in review. The chairman read as follows :—State of footpaths—not done yet, no money. (Laughter). Speed of motor cars—danger signs have been fixed.—Mr. Woodward: They are no use; state of the recreation ground. It was decided that it be left .in the bands of Mr. Woodward to get ,the snid also to get someone to cut the hedges. The chairman then reminded the members of the council that April would soon be here and they had better get something done. (Laughter).

ROSE SHOW.

On Saturday the members of the Penketh and District Rose Society held their first annual show in the Wesleyan School. From the keen interest taken in the event there can be no doubt that this newly-formed Society has come to stay. The sunless weather of June • and early July has been-unfavourable to rose growing, but many of the blooms'on view were perfect", showing very little if any of the unseasonable conditions which hate marred the beauty of gardens generally. They reflected great credit en the growers, all of whom reside in the district. The show was declared open at three o'clock by Mr. James Dodds, who expressed the pleasure it gave him to open their first show.. The room, which had a most pleasing appearance, had been most tastefully decorated by the willing hands of the committee and Mr. J. Woodward, special praise being due to Mr. and Mrs. Woods, of Heath-road, Penketh, who had provided the many flags and streamers lent to him by the gsrod people of Sankey schools. To a few women

Many English women stick to the ways their mothers taught them. But there are a few women who are always on the look-out for better ways of doing things. To those women we have something to say.

The Fels-Naptha Way is a better

way of doing your washing.

All you need to do is to try it next washing day. If you are not satisfied, your grocer returns your money. You keep the soap. So there!

White clothes

Wet the clothes, and soap the soiled parts with Fels-Naptha soap. Roll each piece into a close roll, and place them all in a tub with enough water to cover them. Soak for thirty

Fels - Naptha

The soap with a Way of its own.

minutes.

Then rub in the same ‘vater;..rub a little in the rinsing water; rinse in thc.l, usual way. That's all.

Sir,—l recently attended a Church Army and was much struck by the following statistics Items of- one Year's Church Army Figures. The speakers, when questioned about these statistics, were not ready with an explanation. One said that men were more helpless than women. I should like to ask your readers, who may know more about charity organizations than I do, whether this disparity between charity towards men and women is common, and, if so. how it may be accounted for.— Yours,

S. F. WARING.

Mr. Birrell's Triumph in Ireland. Sir,—We are rightly hearing a great deal just now of the signal and splendid triumph Of Liberal principles as recently applied in South Africa by the granting of self-government to the Transvaal and Orange River Colony. This wise policy, and the development that has now followed it, will surely for all time be reg.rcle os NO of the most memorable events in the ;hole of political history. And now we have at home another illustration of the beneficent working of these same principles which, although on a smaller scale, is in its way equally significant. It is but a few months since the whole of the Tory press was denouncing Mr. Birrell. and his Irish policy up hill and down dale ,—language could scarcely be found to express their vehement indigna- tion against a man and a policy, of which we were told that both the one and the other were the limit; and of couise on the strength of the unhappy agrarian disturbance that then existed there was the old furious, futile claim for coercion. Birrell would not listen to it: he stuck firmly to his guns and his policy. And now what a change. His salary was down for dis- cussion on Wednesday and it was actually voted with- out a division before eight o'clock in the evening! Mr. Long, always an honest critic, agreed that there was a marked improvement in the condition of Ireland, and Mr. Birrell has the immense satisfaction of vindicating • his method of Government by the ordinary law, which lie has insisted on following in Ireland, and therefore of vindicating also both Liberal and Irish Nationalist With your kind permission I will write a few lines next week on the extremely important Debate on Foreign Affairs which we had yesterday.—Yours faithfully,

A. H. CR OSFIELD.

39, Hyde Park Gate, S.W July 23rd, 19M.

HIGH PEAK ELECTION.

Liberal Triumph in Great Budget Fight.

The result of the polling in the High Peak Division of Derbyshire for a member of Parliament to fill the vacancy caused by the appointment of Mr. Oswald Partington to be a Junior Lord of the Treasury, was declared as follows yesterday at Chapel-en-le-Frith : Mr. 0. Partington " 5,619 Mr. A. Profumo 5,272

Liberal majority No change. PREVIOUS ELECTIONS. Former elections have resulted as follows: 1392. 1900. Col. Sidebotham (C) 4,609 0. Partington (L)... 4,591 J. F. Cheetbam (G.L.) 4,243 S. Roberts (C)

Con. majority 366 Lib. majority ... 159 ]895. 1906. Vol. adebottam (C) 4,671 0. Partington (L) 5,450 A. G. Symonds (L) 4,164 A. Profumo (C) 4,662

Con. majority...... 507 Lib majority ... 788 AFTER THE POLL.

After the poll Mr. Partington was carried shoulder high, and followed by 5,000 persons to the Market Place where he addressed the crowd.

He thanked the electors for their magnificent victory. "We have won a great victory in spite of the great influences at work against us in this last few weeks. We have triumphed and we shall triumph again. We have a good cause." "r should like to take this opportunity of thanking Mr. Profumo. Ile has behaved like a gehtleman, and I wish I could say the same of his supporters. Mr. Partington called for three cheers for Mr. Lloyd- George, which were heartily given. Later, at hls• com- mittee rooms, Mr. Partington again complimented Mr. Profumo, and said that the magnificent victory was a message to Mr. Lloyd-George to push forward with his Budget.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The programme presented by the management of the above place of amusement this week is a very 'varied and attractive one and sightly crowds have been the roll during it preentation. The thief and most popular turn on the bill is Harty Levaine, a comedian who has a way of his own. He keeps the audiences in continuous laugher with his patter and funnyosities. There is a ripple of titters and giggles the whole time this extremely popular artiste occu- pies the "boards." A turn which has never been produced before in the town is the champion tight wire performance by James and Jenny Joe. James is extremely clever on the wire, one of his feats being to jump over 6 wicker chairs placed on the wire. In doing this trick he balances himself on the wire at one end of the chairs, takes a mighty leap, clears the obstacle and lands on the wiTt) at the other side retaining his balance all the while. He seems to be quite at home while cycling up and down the narrow track. his marvellous performance on the wire must be seen to be realized. Clifton and Gibson are comedians in song, patter and dance, and provitle a pleasant turn. Rose and Challoner introduce a laughable and up-to-date sketch entitled "The Porter and the Singer." Challoner is no mean artist in the way of mimicry. Marion Scott presents two Australian songs, arranged in the garb of a cow-boy, and gets a good reception. Mimicry has an excellent exponent in Albert Townshend, a society entertainer, who is heartily applauded for his slng Sally in our Alley" and others The Palace Perfection Pictures take. a prominent part this week, and present two highly dramatic scenes entitled " Don Carlos," predicting a• Spanish Court tragedy the "Mystic Arrow." There is also a aide-splitter entitled "The Persistent Lover." A good programme of music is provided by the band under the leaiership of Mr. E Widdop.

===== 0002601_19090724_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME

Another excellently varied programme has been secured for next week. The bill is headed by Alburtus and Millar who vvill present a "Scene at the Fairgrounds." Perrier will give a novelventriloquial act. The other artistes will be :—Tod Lowen, -comedian; Stevens and Brett, refined burlesque entertainers • The Sisters Desmond, character duettists; Minnie Warner, described as- the rubber girl; Little Lily Shaw, the Pocket Vesta Tilley. The Palace Perfection 'Pictures will show" Blind man zind his dog" and "The Impervious tramp."

The enterprising management of this theatre have this week provided their patrons with a complete change from the usual fare. It takes the form of an admirable series of animated pictures, probably the best ever presented in Warrington, and the audiences have been cordial in their approval. The finest set represents " Napoleon, his life and fall " and the outstanding events in the career of that remarkable man are depicted realistically and with great magni- ficence of scenic effect. M. Charley, the famous actor of the Theatre Antoine, Paris, impersonated Napoleon in the original production and his acting is marked with great dramatic 'powers. The scenes thrown on the screen range from the battle of Toulon to the great debacle at Waterloo, the vision of St. Helena, and the deposed Emperor's death. " Eng- land invaded" is the subject for an up to-date series of pictures. Part one represents invasion "as it might be " under the present condition. Members of a peaceable English family are enjoying themselves in the garden of their country home when news arrives that an invading army has landed on our, shores. At first it is received with incredulous laught4r but confirmation is only too speedily forthcoming and one of the young men, armed with ti e single gun which the household boasts, foolishly sallies forth to try his marksmanship against tli6 enemy. Of course, he is shot for his pains and foreign officers take possession of the house. They are weakly resisted by the other male-kind who hastily equipped themselves with bats and other useless weapons but who are soon forced to capitulate and are shot down in cold blood. The women weep and wail and the invaders take an early opportunity of hauling down the Union Jack which flows above the house, now the scene of desolation. Past 2 portrays things as they should be. When the first intimation of the invasion comes to hand, one of the men at once puts himself in telephone communica- tion, presumably with the military authorities, and in an incredibly short space of time he and his brethren emerge from the house clad in military uniform, while the ladies as speedily assume the garb of hospital nurses. As far as can be judged the men after doing a little skirmishing on their own join the troops which have mobilized and the concluding pictures show cavalry and artillery making prepara- tions for combat. An instructive and pleasing feature of the programme is a lecture on "Picturesque America and its wonders" by Professor A. Claude Braden, of Basa,dena, California, who exhibits some exquisite views and presents a mass of useful inform- ation in an attractive form. An exceptionatly clever ventriloquial entertainment is given by Tom Franks and creates much merriment. There are many laugh- able sets of pictures and the programme goes witk a capital swing throughout. The orchestral selections, under Mr. Norman Latham's conductorship, prove very popular. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

A melodrama of exceptional power and interest, is "The Great Awakening," which is to be presented to Warrington audiences at the Royal Court Theatre next week. The cast includes Miss Kate Sydney, a well- known actress,whose part in the play is very exacting. She has achieved a remarkable success in- the character. Miss Sydney is supported by an excellent company. AN INVASION PLAY.

Mr. B. S. Townroe, of Warrington, has written a military drama in four acts entitled "A Nation in Arms"and it will be performed for the first time at War- rington during the week commencing September 20th. In compliance with the usual/legal formality, the play was produced for copyright purposes only at the Royal. Court Theatre on Thursday afternoon, amongst those who read parts being the author, Captain Crosfield, Captain Haslam, Mrs. Haslam, Mr. A. M. Loader, and Mr. N. Latham. The plot of the play is an adaptation from a short story of the Franco- Prussian war written by M. Emile Zola. It gives a picture of the effect of war upon, the inhabitants of a Yorkshire fishing village, at which an expeditionary force of the aggressors have landed. There will be many exciting incidents, including the defence of an orchard by Territorials and the midnight escape of the hero from a window. Special scenery has been painted by Mr. Walter Johnstone and the military costumes will be supplied by Mews. Morris Angel and Co. The overture and incidental music have been specially composed by Miss Daisy Sefi. The object of the play is, of course, to create the feeling of the necessity for home defence, and in order that the production shall lack nothing from the artistic standpoint, a strong company has been selected from well-known London managements and includes artistes Mr. Arthur Bouchier's, Mr. Seymour Hicks', Mr. Granville Barker's, Mr. George Edwardes' and Mr. Frederick Harrison's Company, each artiste being carefully selected for am individual part. Miss Gwendolen Logan, who plays Jessie Fisher was formerly a member of Mrs. Patrick Campbell's and Mr. George Alexander's company. She was seen acting in " Chips " at the Haymarket, London, last month, and within three hours was secured for leading lady in "A Nation in Arms." The company will also include Mr. C. Leveson Lane, who supported Miss Evelyn Millard in the original productions of " Idols " and "The Real Woman " ; Mr. Alfred Bristowe and Mr. Stafford Hilliard, both the last three years members of Mr. Bourchier's company at the Garrick. Mr. Stafford Hilliard, who will also stage manage the production, is an old college friend of the author s. After the play has been produced at Warrington, performances will be given in the principle cities and towns of the North of England and also on the South Coast.

HOLIDAY ANNOUNCEMENTS.

(Continued from page 2). A SIX DAYS' CRUISE. In tlese days of pleasant sea cruises, we would draw the attention of our readers to our advertising columns, in Nvhich-the Tedcastle Line announce a cruise of six days by their favourite steamer" Cumbria" leaving Collingwood Doc't on Saturday. 31st inst. at 9a .m. for Whitehaven and Maryport, giving ample time to visit some of our English lakes before proceeding from Whitehaven on the Wednesday for Dublin. She will leave Dublin again at 4 p.m. on Friday the 6th August, arriving here in the small hours of Saturday morning. Tourists have about a day and a half in Dublin, and as they are allowed to sleep on board during the whole trip, the inclusive fare of two guineas, covering 14 meals is most economical, and the trip most varied and interesting. Except on the Sunday, dinner, however, is not provided, so as to leave ample time for sight- seeing. Hand bills giving full particulars can be obtained from the Tedcastle Line, who also despatch their fast s.s. "Blackrock "for Dublin from south side Nelson Dock on Friday the 30th inst. at 8 p.m. return- ing from Dublin on Monday 2nd August at 5 p.m. the return saloon fare being only 10s. L. Sc N.-.W. EXCURSIONS. - -

The excursions arranged by the London and North- Western Railway cover a very wide field on its far reaching system. To those visiting Ireland, it will be well known that the North-Western Railway Company's route presents such remarkable facilities that con- sideration is absolutely unnecessary. It is an unques- tionable fact that Ireland, with its richly wooded glens. its mountain sides wild and abundant with golden gorse, and its traditional charm, impresses the traveller so much that the memory of such a tour will never fade with the passing of time. This Company, recognising the wonderful and varied charms of Nord' Wales, have made elaborate arrangements for the convenience of the traveller who chooses that place for his relaxa- tion. Beautiful and varied as is Nature's wonderful panorama there are at these Welsh resorts the finest promenades, public gardens, and theatres to be found at any of the Continental resorts. Rhyl. Colwyn Bay, Llandudno, Bangor, are seaside resorts which are well- known to our readers both by viiitation and reputation, and those people whose knowledge of them has only been gained from the word pictures of others would be well advised to visit North Wales, and revel in an earthly paradise, by sun bathed sands, and in exhilarating sea and mountain breezes, return like a giant refreshed ready to undertake the most onerous task with consummate ease. In addition to the above mentioned places the North-Western Company have arranged trips to Scotland, tho West of England, Blackpool, Carlisle, South and Central Wales, Man- , chester: York and the North-Eastern Stations, London, Birmingham, Windermere and the Lakes, and other towns and resorts. Before deciding where to go intend- ing passengers should consult the London and North- Western Railway Company's pamphlets which are com- piled and issued in a very artistic manner.

THE ROYAL VISIT.

SPECIAL MEETING OF THE COUNCIL DEMANDED.

Cost of the Necklau.

As a sequel to the recent Royal visit to Warrington and the sdarcity of informa- tion regarding the presentation to her Royal Highness Princess Victoria of a diamond necklace, fifteen members of the Town Council have signed the following requisition to the Mayor :

Warrington, July 13th, 1909. 7 To his Worship the Mayor, Alderman W. J. Forshaw.

We, the undersigned members of the Town Council of the County Borough of Warring- ton hereby request you to convene a special ;meeting of the Council to consider the minutes of the Council of the sth July, 1909, and all circumstances relating thereto, and to pass such resolutions thereon as-the Council may deem necessary.

LOCAL NEWS. 1

===== 0002601_19090724_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
JOHN LORD F. T. LASHFORD G. W. JOSEPH THOS. H. ASHCROFT J. HALLIWELL JAMES T. LOCKER THOS. CHANTLER JOS. HAWTHORN J. DICKINSON JAS. SKELLAND JACOB SANKEY JNO. WILKINSON THOS. BARTON H. SINGLETON ELIJAH CUNNINGTON.

We understand that the minutes which it is especially desired to discuss are those sanctioning the preSentation to Princess Victoria of a suitable gift in commemoration of her birthday and appointing the following committee to select the article :—Coun- cillors Locker, Pemberton, Wilkinson, H. Roberts and Sankey. As a matter of fact, this committee was never called together,hut the presentation of a diamond necklace was made to Princess Victoria on the occasion of the Royal visit, on the day following the Council meeting. The Mayor has refused to call a special meeting, and it remains to be seen whether the members who have signed the requisition will take the further step of cal4g a meeting on their own account.

MEETING OF THE RECEPTION COMMITTEE.

A meeting of the • Reception Committee was held at the Town Hall yesterday morn- ing, the Mayor (Alderman Forshaw) presid- ing. A statement of accounts was sub- mitted which showed a balance in hand after payment of all the accounts incurred by the committee, and including the sum of £155 for the diamond necklace presented to Princess Victoria. The surplus of the £1,200 originally voted to the Mayor, which remains after paying expenses, will be returned to the Council.

The committee passed a vote 'of thanks to the Mayor and 'congratulated him upon the success of the proceedings. The opinion was expressed that the expenditure incurred was most economical.

The Committee approved a !report to the Council placing on record the official pro- ceedings in connection with the visit of the King. The report states :—Your Committee desire to express their appreciation of the work done by the several sub-committees and their thanks to each individual member thereof.

-The thanks of your commit tee are due to Sir Gilbert Greenall Bart., for his kindness and generosity in lending to your committee the two stands which were erected in front of the Town Hall.

The thanks of your committee are also due to Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons Lid., for their kindness and generosity in erect- ing the large stand in front of the Education Offices and Garven House.

The Decorating Sub-Committee divided the route of the Royal visit into three sections, and on their recommendation the committee accepted the tenders of the following firms for the decorations of the streets : Mr. T. Prince per Messrs. C. T. Brock and Co., Messrs. R. Garnett and Sons, Messrs. J. and W. Dutton per Messrs. Fred Wilkins and Bro. Ltd., and the three firms carried out the work entrusted to them very satisfactorily. In fact your committee desire to express their unstinted praise of the artistic decora- tions and the beautiful effect and appearance of the route on the day of the King's visit. Your Committee also desire to thank the inhabitants and householders along the line of route for their hearty and loyal response to our appeal to decorate their premises. The effect of the decorations generally gave the liveliest satisfaction to the inhabitants and visitors to the town.

The report then records the events of the day, the form of the loyal address and the King's reply, and the letters since received from the Right Hon. R. B. Haldane and Lord Derby. The form of the address was submitted to Lord Knollys, the King's private secretary, and after approval by him it was sent to the Secretary of State who formally assented to the address in the terms submitted being presented to his Majesty. The address which was jointly executed by three of the students of the School of Art, viz :—S. W. Clatworthy, A. Cooke and A. Hayward, was an exquisite piece of art and reflected great credit on the students named. Your committee also wish to place on record that the opportunity was taken of Presenting through the Mayor, on behalf of the inhabitants, a beautiful diamond neck- lace to Princess Victoria, who accompanied their Majesties and whose birthday happily coincided with the day of the Royal visit. Her Royal Highness was graciously pleased to accept the gift, and has expressed her pleasure and satisfaction in receiving it as shown in the letter from Mr. Haldane. Suitable arrangements were made for the school children by the Director of Education and his staff (to whom our thanks are due.) The Committee also express their thanks to Lieutenant-Colonel Fairclough and the officers, non-commissioned offiCers and men of the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment for their services in lining the route and for providing the guard of honour, and also to Major Edridge and the officers, non-commissioned officers and men of the 8/40th Regimental District for their services in lining the route. _

Your- Committee are glad to be able to report that his Worship has discharged all the liabilities incurred by your Committee. A word of praise is due to the Chief Constable for the excellent arrangements he made for preserving order in the streets.

Our thanks are also due to the Town Clerk and his staff, and to other officials of the Corporation for the able and willing assist- ance rendered to your Committee.

Reduction of Railway Workers. The London and North-Western Railway Company are reducing their staff of railway carriage workers. Notices have been served for the discharge of over fifty men at the carriage shops at Crewe, and a similar number at the carriage works at Wolverton. The other ,employees are working reducedhours. The National Independent Order of Odd-

fellows. The half-yearly meeting of the" Friendship" Lodge National-Independent Order of Oddfellows, was held at the Blue Lion Inn, Market-place, on Monday even- ing. N.J. Bro. J. J, Perry) presented a beautiful emblem of merit to the retiring Grand Master (Bro. R. Illidge) and spoke of the excellent manner in which he had attended to his duties. A Carter's Compensation.

An application or arbitration under the Workmen's Compensation Act was heard at the Warrington County Court on Thursday last, before Mr. Deputy Judge Oulton, between James Washington, of Philip- street, Warrington, and Edward Isherwood, Tanner's- lane, contractor. Mr. Arthur Browne appeared for the applicant and Mr. F. E. Bodel (instructed by Mr. Thomas S. Steel) appeared for the respondent.—Mr. Browne said that the applicant had met with an accident wising out of his employment and claimed half his average weekly wages whilst incapacitated. He was a driver and was engaged by the respondent at £1 per week. He had worked for Mr. Isherwood for some time. On April 7th he was out with a horse and lurry when he was run over by the lurry.— Applicant bore out Mr. Browne's statement, but in cross-examination admitted that he was only employed for two or three days a week and that his average weekly wage would only be about 15s. per week.— His Honour said that after hearing the applicant's evidence he was quite satisfied that there was only casual employment and he would give a verdict for the applicant for 7s. fid. a week with costs on Scale B.

TENNIS, CROQUET, AND GARDEN FURNITUE.— J. S. GARLAND, Bridge st., Warrington.--Advt.

Domestic Servants Sued. A case of interest to those who employ domestic servants was heard at the Warrington County Court on Thursday, when Thomas H. Pretty of Padgate sued Alice Simcock and Jane Rees, domestic servants for damages for breach of contract. Mr. C. H. Unsworth (for Mr. Thomas S. Steel) appeared for the plaintiff. Mr. Unsworth said the defendants were servants in the plaintiffs employ up to a month ago, when one morning they suddenly left the house. Simcock was on a week's notice and Rees a month on trial but neither of them had made any complaints and he vas at a loss to understand the cause of their going. He said the plaintiff did not bring the ease for any other reason than that of principle. He had been very badly treated by girls of this kind and he thought that he would put a stop to it. Plaintiff bore out Mr. Unsworth's statements and the defendant Rees stated that she left on account of the washing, and Simcock that she did not Avant to stafwhen her sister, who had also been in the plaintiff's employ had left. Mr. Registrar Ridgway, in giving judgment for •the plaintiff said he did not see any legitimate reason for their leaving in the manner they had and they would have to pay, the defendant Rees £1 Gs. and the defendant Simcock 10s. each for damages;

• Warrington Show. The twentieth annual Show of the 'Warrington and District Agricultural Society will be held in Walton Park on Thursday, August 19th, when prizes, cups and medals of the value of £7OO will be competed for. Tuesday, August 10th, is the last day for entries. Stockton Heath Choral Union. At the invitation of Mrs. Lyon, the members of the Stockton Heath Choral Union visited Appleton Hall on Saturday evening and sang before the guests whom Mr. and Mrs. Lyon were entertaining. The programme included "He watching over Israel, "0 happy eyes," "Sweet and Low "and " Here, by Babylon's Wave," and all the items were appreciatively received. Mr. Harry Berrey conducted. At the close Mrs. Lyon expressed her thanks to the choir and their talented conductor for the pleasure they had afforded the company and also stated how delighted she was to hear of their recent competitive success. The members of the choir were conducted round the beautiful grounds and greatly enjoyed their visit: to the hall.

Warrington Cycling Club. The run to Piekmere on Saturday turned out to be one of the most enjoyable runs the club has had this season, and the afternoon was favoured by beautiful weather. Not a large number put in an attendance but those members who did turn out had a most varied and enjoyable afternoon. A start from head- quarters was made to time and the destination was reached via various routes early in the afternoon. The stay was taken up by boating, bowls and other forms of recreation. "Tea was served at the Red Lion Hotel, and ample justice was done to it by the wheelers. The return home was made by Tabley and Lymm and Warrington was reached early in the evening. The bowling handicaps at Sankey which have been in pro- gress since the beginning. of the season were cora= pleied on Wednesday evening. On Thursday after- noon the wheelers met the Warrington Thursday in a cricket match which turned out to be a -very interesting fixture. On account of the unsettled weather the run lb Glazebury on Thursday was not well attended. Members, however, had a very enjoy- able game of bowls during their stay. The club's arrangements for the coming week are: To-day's run to Sandiway (President's day) ; bowling at Sankey on Wednesday,; Thursday's run to Tabley. Rechabite Picnics. On Saturday the Wycliffe Juvenile Order of Rechabites held their annual trip to Overton, Fred - sham, and to the- pleasure grounds of Mrs. Parker Hoose. About 140 people left Bank Quay at 1-5 accompanied by several menibers of The Adult Tent including Bro. Colley (Chief Ruler), Bro. Wright (Deputy Ruler), Bro. Howard (Adult Tent Secretary) and others. Frodsham was reached about two o'clock when all made their way to Mersey View to partake in games and rambles round the hill. About 4.30 the party sat down for tea, which everyone did full justice to. "After tea further games etc. were arranged for children and time was soon called to be en route for home, which was reached shortly after ninop'clock. During the afternoon the party had their photograph taken. Beautiful Weather favoured the party, and they all enjoyed themselves. The arrangements were carried out by officers of the Juvenile Tent, Bro. ,Garner (superintendent), Bro. J. A. Bradley (secre- tary), and members of the Adult Tent.—The annual picnic of "John Dimond" juvenile tent, No. 914, also took place to Frodsham. Over 200 members left Bank Quay Station by special train at 1.30 p.m., returning from Frodsham at 8.40 p.m. After tea, sports had been arranged, the following members being successful in carrying off the prizes :—Girls : 40 yards flat race, 1, E. Smith; 2, M. Trina, 3, M, Sutton, 50 yards flat race: 1, M. Ellis ; 2, E. •Epruce ; 3, E. Hardman. Ball race: I,M. Percival; 1, J. Davies; 3, ,F. Mayer. Egg and spoon race : 1, E. Wilson. Boys.-50 yards flat race : 1, T. Davies; 2, F. Holford ; 3, H Spruce. 60 yards flat race : • 1, T. Percival; 2, T. Littlewood ; 3, E. Wright. 100 yards flat race: 1, G, T. Jones; 2, G. Foden ; 3, J. Hewitt.

Warrington Children's Summer Camp.

One hundred and three poor children (girls) set off from the Drill' Hall on Saturday morning, shortly after !en o'clock, for the camp at Prestatyn. They were in the charge of seven ladies :—Mrs. Whiteley, Mrs. Burge, Misses Jones, Povey, Jefferson and Peers, officers ; Miss Emson, superintendent. The weathei, contrary to expectations, has been delightful, although a strong wind has prevailed during the greater part of the stay. On Sunday morning the children were taken to their respective churches and the remainder of the day was spent quietly in the camp ground and on the shore. The children waded on Monday along the shore to see the pierrots. The entertainment was enjoyed very much and the choruses have since been heard all around the camp. A concert on the shore given by the children, ended the day. Dyserth Falls were visited on Tuesday morning and excited great wonder and admiration. The return journey was made through the woods on the mountain side and all were quite ready for dinner when they reached " home." - On Wednesday the children walked to Prestatyn Mountain and came back through Meliden where they were anxious to hear the. Meliden school children who were out at play,talk in their native tongue. The pierrots were again visited on Thursday. The 'children, on Friday assisted in preparing their huts for the next hundred who will arrive to-day. Several ladies and gentlemen have visited the camp during the week, one being the Rev. David Card, vicar of Birmingham. The following is his note as entered in the visitors' book, Julynth, 1909 I have visited the camp with great pleasure. 'pie people of War- rington should be proud of it, I wish heartily Birming- ham and other great cities had similar institutions for their poor boys and girls.— David Card, vicar of St. Edward's, Birmingham." A lady visitor from Man- chester bought three of the children a pair of boots each, which are thankfully received. _ The children who were looking bronzed and much better in health, 'were all sorry to leave the camp when departing time came.—lt is announced that the annual picnic to the camp will take place on Tuesday, August 3rd, when the Mayor (Alderman Forshaw). will open the new administration offices. The train will leave Arpley station at 12.25 mid-day. Subscribers and friends are cordially invited. Tickets, inclusive of railway fare, luncheon and tea, will be ss. each.
